# LudensEngine Benchmark Report

Generated by `pixi run benchmark`. Each table is a cross matrix produced
by running one identical workload across interchangeable implementations,
validating that LudensEngine's swap seams (ECS `StorageBackend`, collision
`BroadPhase`/`NarrowPhase`) are both correct (see the parity tests) and
performance-competitive. Timings are wall-clock ns via `perf_counter_ns`
with `keep()` guarding against dead-code elimination; absolute numbers are
machine-specific, so read the *relative* columns.

Host: CPU "Intel(R) Xeon(R) Processor @ 2.10GHz", 4 logical cores, accelerator none detected (generated 2026-11-12 by scripts/run_benchmarks.sh). Absolute numbers and the parallel-scaling curves depend on these; rows from a host with a different fingerprint are not comparable.


## Engine implementations — movement workload

Note the two archetype rows: `archetype (handle)` is the ergonomic
`query2 + get/set` path (allocates a List per query, walks the entity index
per access); `archetype (soa)` is the column path via `query2_views`. The SoA
row is ~10x faster than the handle row — the handle path, not ECS storage, is
what made the first version of this benchmark look slow.

## Collision algorithms

The narrowphase table includes the CGA rows: sphere-sphere answered by
conformal-algebra inner products vs the analytic euclidean test (parity in
`test_cga_narrowphase`) — the price of the GA abstraction, measured.

The final table settles the HETEROGENEOUS case, which is the one the
"unified branchless meet" argument is actually about: the claim is that one
algebraic incidence test beats a traditional dispatcher full of per-shape-pair
branches. Both paths hold spheres and planes in one registry over identical
candidate pairs; the control is a hand-written euclidean switch. Plane count
is the scaling axis, because it controls how often the dispatch changes
branch, and the planes are interleaved through the cloud rather than left in
one contiguous run (a run is trivially predicted and would flatter the branchy
path).

CGA loses at every mix, by **2.4x-3.2x**, and the ratio does not improve as
the scene gets more heterogeneous. Two reasons, and the first is the more
important one: `CgaShapeNarrowPhase` **still branches on shape kind**, exactly
like the euclidean dispatcher. The uniformity CGA buys is in the FORMULA
(a sphere-sphere and a sphere-plane test are both grade-1 inner products), not
in the dispatch — so there is no branch elimination to collect, and what
remains is the cost of carrying 32-float multivectors instead of 4 floats.
A genuinely branch-free version would need one uniform representation and one
code path for every pairing, which this algebra layer does not currently
provide.

Note the hit counts differ by ~0.3% between the two paths. That is a second
result rather than noise: the euclidean path measures the centre distance
directly, while CGA reconstructs it as d² = r₁²+r₂²−2·S₁·S₂ — a subtraction of
similar magnitudes — so a few pairs sitting exactly on the touching boundary
fall on the other side. The abstraction costs a little conditioning as well as
a little speed.

## Collision — 3D signed distance fields

The engine's existing SDF layer is 2D, so this is a 3D layer rather than a
port of a working one — worth stating because the roadmap assumed otherwise.
Contact is found by driving the two fields to a common value, and the
quantity that IS the penetration is the SUM of the fields where they agree,
not the minimum of their maximum: for two spheres that minimum is half the
overlap, because the balance point splits the gap. `test_sdf3` pins the
converged answer against the analytic sphere pair — 115/115 on hit
classification and ~1e-7 on depth.

Against a closed form the SDF is **~20x slower**, which is the price of
finding a contact rather than deriving it. Note the iteration cap barely
matters (8, 16 and 32 all land at ~94 ns): for unit-gradient primitive fields
the descent closes the gap in one step and exits early, so the cap only binds
on CSG fields, which are a bound on distance rather than the distance.

The CSG row has no analytic row beside it, and that absence is the result. A
box with a sphere bitten out of it is answered by the same code path at ~4x
the primitive cost, where the analytic narrowphase cannot express the shape
at all. Contact cost also stops depending on tessellation, since there is no
mesh in between.

Still to do: this lives at narrowphase level and is NOT yet wired into
`solver6`, which dispatches on a shape-kind tag and needs a multi-point
manifold rather than a single deepest contact. Convex hulls took exactly that
route and are now wired (kind 3, see the manifold table); an SDF shape kind
would follow the same pattern, with the clipping step replaced by sampling the
field around the deepest point.

### 3D SDF contact vs the analytic pairs

## Collision — manifold narrowphase (contact patch vs boolean test)

What the solver actually pays: `test_manifold` produces clipped contact
points with per-point depths (parity vs the boolean paths in `test_manifold`),
next to its boolean twin on the same pairs.

The last two rows are the convex-hull path (`collision/hull.mojo`, wired into
`ContactScene6` as shape kind 3), and the `pts=` totals are the point of the
table. GJK+EPA reports one point per hit; the hull path reports four — the same
patch the specialised AABB clipper produces, on shapes the AABB clipper cannot
express. That is what the ~2.5x costs buy, and it is not a micro-optimisation:
a single-point manifold applies no torque about the contact, so a box resting
on it rocks and eventually topples. The patch is why a hull box now settles
within 1e-6 of the height an identical native box settles at (`test_hull`).

Face enumeration is reported separately because it amortizes differently: it is
O(V^3) over the vertex cloud and runs ONCE per shape at construction, while the
manifold runs once per pair per step. Summing them would hide that the
expensive half is paid at load time. At V=8 it is ~3.1 us per shape; the cubic
term means a 64-vertex hull costs roughly 500x that, so hulls are meant to be
built once and instanced, not authored per frame.

## Collision — static level geometry (midphase)

A level is not a convex body, so until `collision/trimesh.mojo` it could not
be expressed at all. The contact is not new — a triangle IS a convex hull with
three vertices and one face, so the hull narrowphase above handles it — but a
level has thousands of triangles and a crate touches three, and the step that
turns the first number into the second is the midphase.

Three ways to do it, on identical terrain: brute over every triangle, a SAH BVH
over triangle boxes (`TriMesh`), and cell arithmetic on a regular grid
(`HeightField`). Read the `cand=` totals alongside the times: a midphase that
is fast because it hands MORE triangles to the narrowphase has moved the cost
rather than removed it. All three return the same count here, and
`test_trimesh` gates the heightfield and the explicit soup to the same
resting height on the same surface.

The sweep is what the table is for. Across a 16.8x growth in triangle count the
brute path grows in step with it — linear, as it must. The BVH grows by rather
less than half again, which is the tree getting deeper. The heightfield barely
moves: its query is clamped arithmetic on cell indices, so the size of the
terrain never enters it, and what little drift remains is cache behaviour on a
larger height array rather than more work. That is
the trade it is FOR, and it is paid for in expressiveness — a heightfield cannot
describe an overhang, a wall, or a cave, and a level that needs one has no
choice but the tree.

The second table is the honest end of it: at 7,938 triangles and 64 crates the
midphase is a small enough share of a full solver step that the two are
indistinguishable. The midphase choice starts to matter at terrain sizes where
the brute column has already become unusable.

## Collision — mesh baking and mesh SDFs (17.45)

A collision mesh is baked once at load: vertex weld, repair, quadric
simplification and active-edge flags (`geometry.mesh_bake`). The flags are what
stop ghost collisions: in `test_active_edges` a frictionless box sliding over a
floor cut into 400 quads comes to a stop without them (0.03 m/s left of 2) and
keeps 2.0000055 m/s with them. The second table prices the mesh SDF: one
trilinear sample against a scan of every triangle; the grid's bake is the cost
paid up front (brute force over triangles here).

## Collision — filtering and contact events

Layers, masks, sensors (`set_filter` / `set_sensor`) and the began/stay/ended
event stream, all in `ContactScene6`. The filter test is two ANDs at the top of
`_try_pair`, the one point both the brute and the broadphase enumeration funnel
through, so the two seams cannot disagree about what was filtered —
`test_filter_events` gates them to bit-identical results AND to an identical
event stream.

A first version of this table compared one crate pile against the same pile with
layers assigned and made filtering look like a 45% slowdown. That was two
different SCENES: filtered crates interpenetrate, so the pile settles somewhere
else. The rows below instead start from the case filtering is FOR — two
populations sharing one volume, which is what a player layer and an enemy layer
standing in the same room actually is.

Read the `asleep=` column before drawing a conclusion from the times. The gap
is not contact count (254 against 192 is nowhere near the measured factor): the
filtered scene settles and all 192 crates sleep, while the unfiltered one has
crates spawned inside each other that are pushed apart, drift back, and are
pushed again, so 104 are still awake and being solved. Filtering does not make
contact resolution faster. It is the difference between a scene that comes to
rest and one that never does.

Events are off by default and the last two rows are why: the diff sorts the
contact set every step. On these scenes that is under 3%, which is small but not
nothing for a feature a scene may never read.

### Collision filtering: two populations sharing one volume

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|
| unfiltered (both populations collide) contacts=252 asleep=74 | 192 | step | 4837212.15 | 0.00 | 290.23 |
| filtered (each ignores the other) contacts=192 asleep=192 | 192 | step | 632247.35 | 0.00 | 37.93 |
| filtered + events contacts=192 asleep=192 | 192 | step | 656263.63 | 0.00 | 39.38 |
| unfiltered + events contacts=252 asleep=74 | 192 | step | 6387976.23 | 0.00 | 383.28 |


## Geometry — exact predicates (robustness layer)

Every hull, clip and simplex routine ends in a SIGN question, and when the true
determinant is near zero the rounding error can exceed it — so the answer is not
imprecise, it is wrong, and it can be wrong INCONSISTENTLY: the same three points
reported as a left turn and a right turn depending on the order they arrive in.
A hull builder given contradictory answers does not produce a slightly wrong
hull, it produces one that is not convex.

`geometry/predicates.mojo` answers in stages: float32 with an error bound,
float64 with an error bound, then exact evaluation in error-free expansions
(Dekker/Knuth two-product and two-sum). Exact means exact — for float32 inputs
the returned sign is the sign of the real determinant, because widening a
float32 to a float64 is lossless.

The two regimes are reported separately because they are different numbers.
On generic input the filter decides and robustness is indistinguishable from the
naive sign test — close enough that which one wins moves between runs, so read
the two as equal rather than reading a sign into the gap. On input constructed
to be exactly degenerate the fallback runs every time, and orient2d costs an
order of magnitude over the naive test while orient3d costs several hundred
times it — the
expansion sum there is written as the textbook Leibniz formula over all 24
permutations, chosen for being readable against the definition, since it runs on
a vanishing fraction of calls. incircle and insphere are only ever timed on the
exact path, which is why their rows look expensive: there is no cheap case in
them to average against.

`convex_hull_2d` routes its side test through the layer (`exact=False` keeps
the old one as a seam variant). Both hull rows report their own convexity, and
on this cloud BOTH come out convex — the naive test survives it. What the exact
path buys is not a better answer on one input but the absence of an input where
the answer contradicts itself: `test_predicates` measures the naive test
violating cyclic invariance on about 28% of near-degenerate triples, and the
exact one on none.

| orient2d robust (generic, filter decides) | 20000 | orient2d | 15.93 | 62.77 | 0.32 |

| orient2d robust (degenerate, exact runs) | 20000 | orient2d | 256.15 | 3.90 | 5.12 |

## Scheduler — automatic dependency job graph

`SequentialScheduler` runs systems in registration order and puts the burden of
correctness on whoever wrote the list. `JobGraphScheduler` derives the order
instead: each system declares its component read and write sets as bitmasks, two
systems conflict when one writes what the other reads or writes, and everything
that does not conflict shares a level.

Within a level no two systems write the same component, so the outcome cannot
depend on the order they run in — which is why the result is BIT-IDENTICAL to
the sequential scheduler rather than merely equivalent. `test_jobgraph` gates
that on two storage backends, serial and fanned out.

Deriving the schedule is timed on its own because it happens once at
construction, not per tick; quoting it per tick would flatter it by however many
ticks the game runs. Running the derived schedule serially costs about 1% over
registration order, which is the price of deciding the order at run time.

The speedup is capped by the workload's shape, not by the core count: four of
the six systems can overlap, so Amdahl allows at most 2x, and the measured
figure is below that because fanning out four short systems does not reach four
times the throughput. That is the real ceiling on SYSTEM-level parallelism and
the reason the solver also parallelises within a system.

The honest weak point is stated in the module: nothing verifies that a system's
declaration matches what its `apply` actually touches. A wrong declaration
produces a wrong schedule — the same trade every DOTS-style scheduler makes.

levels: 3  width0: 4  width1: 1
digests equal: True
### Automatic dependency scheduling (6 systems, 4000 entities)

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|
| derive schedule (construction only) | 6 | build | 242.23 | 4.13 | 0.24 |
| sequential | 4000 | tick | 1607478.84 | 0.00 | 321.50 |
| job graph (serial within level) | 4000 | tick | 1661947.79 | 0.00 | 332.39 |
| job graph (levels fanned out) | 4000 | tick | 1178602.42 | 0.00 | 235.72 |


## Numerics — global solve vs local iteration

Every other solver in this engine iterates LOCALLY — PBD projection, PGS,
Jacobi, per-vertex Newton — and every one is stiffness bound: information
travels one neighbour per sweep, so the step size is capped by the stiffest
element. `numerics/` is the global-solve half: CSR matrices, a matrix-free
operator interface, and conjugate gradients.

The first table gives CG and a Jacobi sweep the SAME iteration budget on the
same 1D Poisson system, so the comparison is per unit of work. Jacobi is about
1.5x cheaper per iteration and that is the least interesting number in the
table: read the `res=` column instead. CG reaches 5e-17; Jacobi is still at
0.90 at n=64 and 0.98 at n=1024, which is to say it has not converged at all.

The second table is CG's REAL scale axis, which is the condition number rather
than the size. Two systems of identical size: one with a constant diagonal,
one whose diagonal spans 1e6. Jacobi preconditioning is a scalar multiple of
the identity on the first and does exactly nothing — same iteration count,
same time. On the second it goes from 195 iterations to 84. Reporting only the
second would make the preconditioner look universally worthwhile, and it is
not; a preconditioner that helped everywhere would be a measurement error.

The third table is what the whole package is for. `FemBody.step_implicit`
solves backward Euler matrix-free through CG (no global stiffness matrix is
ever assembled). On a soft beam it costs ~3.7x the explicit step and both are
stable, so the explicit path wins. On a stiff one at the same dt the explicit
row is marked DIVERGED — it is a cost with no result attached, and that is the
comparison: not a faster step, an available one.

### Global solve vs local iteration (1D Poisson, matched budget)

|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|

| jacobi iters=32 res=0.9029835 | 64 | solve | 45070.25 | 0.02 | 9.01 |
| cg iters=128 res=5.2146263e-17 | 256 | solve | 1044224.30 | 0.00 | 52.21 |
| jacobi iters=128 res=0.9508278 | 256 | solve | 708144.98 | 0.00 | 35.41 |
| cg iters=512 res=5.4970245e-17 | 1024 | solve | 17369763.80 | 0.00 | 86.85 |
| jacobi iters=512 res=0.97524184 | 1024 | solve | 11638312.00 | 0.00 | 58.19 |

## Numerics deepening — BiCGStab, dense refinement, gradcheck (17.84)

`cg` / `pcg` assume a symmetric positive definite operator. The first table
solves an upwind convection-diffusion system, which is not symmetric:
BiCGStab converges and its error against the known answer is in the row; CG
runs its whole budget and its error is the reason it is labelled the wrong
solver. The diagonal is constant, so Jacobi is only a rescaling, yet at
n=1024 the Jacobi run diverges where the plain one converges: BiCGStab's
convergence on this non-normal matrix is irregular (GMRES, which is
monotone, is not in the package yet). BiCGStab reports the TRUE residual of the returned f32 answer, not
its recursive estimate (on a convection-only variant the recursion met 1e-6
with an answer off by 6e9, `test_numerics_deep`).

The second table is the price of accuracy in the small dense solve: three
rounds of mixed-precision refinement (residual in f64, correction by the same
f32 elimination) and the exact 1-norm condition number. The third is one
shared `gradcheck` call: 6 controls x 6 step sizes x 2 rollouts.

## Animation — clips, blending and cross-fade

The skinning maths existed; the runtime around it did not. `procedural/anim.mojo`
adds uniformly-sampled clips, three blend modes, and a player that cross-fades
between clips under whatever drives it — `test_anim` drives it from
`procedural/fsm.mojo`.

Costs are per BONE per FRAME and are flat in the bone count, which is what they
should be: the work is per bone and nothing here is quadratic. The three blend
modes are ordered by what they guarantee and come out in the same order by
cost. Linear is cheapest and is NOT constant angular speed — measured at 0.283
where constant speed puts 0.319, an 11% lag a quarter of the way through a
150-degree blend. The geodesic mode (exp/log on the motor manifold) is the only
constant-speed one, at about 3.7x linear.

One detail worth recording because it made an earlier version of the test
vacuous: at exactly halfway, normalised-lerp and slerp are IDENTICAL, since the
normalised midpoint of a chord lies on the great-circle midpoint. A comparison
taken at w = 0.5 shows the two blend modes agreeing to seven digits and proves
nothing about either.

The player rows show what a cross-fade costs: 3.4x a single clip, because a
fading player samples two clips and blends them instead of sampling one.

## Animation transitions: fade vs inertialization (17.64)

`procedural.anim_state.AnimGraph` runs a transition one of two ways: fade
(sample both clips, blend) or inertialization (sample the new clip only,
add the pose offset recorded at the switch, decaying). Both end exactly on
the new clip (`test_anim_runtime`). The sweep is over the share of
characters inside a transition, since that is where the two differ.

## Animation — K-influence skinning and morph targets (17.65)

`skin_motor_k` / `skin_lbs_k` take K (index, weight) pairs per vertex; K = 2
equals the existing `skin_motor` bit for bit (both call one non-inlined
per-vertex function: inlined separately, the same expression rounded
differently in 41 of 500 vertices). `bind_palette` gives the world x
inverse-bind buffer a host shader consumes; `MorphSet` stores sparse
deltas, applied before skinning. Under a 0..170-degree twist across four
bones DLB keeps a vertex at radius 0.2 from the axis, LBS pulls it to 0.097

## Physics — cloth self-collision

Cloth that does not collide with itself can be a flag and cannot be a garment.
`physics/self_collide.mojo` adds vertex-vertex repulsion under a thickness,
hooked into BOTH cloth solvers — XPBD and VBD are two variants of one seam, and
a capability present in only one stops them being comparable.

Read the `gap=` column first: it is the distance between the closest pair of
particles that are NOT spring-connected. On a sheet piling on the floor it is
0.0003 without the feature — the sheet is passing through itself — and 0.060
with it, against a thickness of 0.06. VBD reaches 0.050 rather than the full
thickness, because its repulsion runs after the colour sweeps rather than inside
the per-vertex Newton step and the next sweep pulls some of it back. That is
reported rather than tuned away.

The cost is 12.6x for XPBD and 7.0x for VBD on a piled sheet. The last pair of
rows is a larger sheet run for fewer steps, so it has not piled and the gap is
IDENTICAL either way — that row isolates the cost of looking (a hash rebuild and
27-cell queries finding nothing) from the cost of resolving, which is what a
scene of cloth that rarely folds actually pays.

Topological neighbours are excluded, and that exclusion is what keeps the
feature from being harmful: a particle's springs already hold it at rest length
from its grid neighbours, and repelling those too would fight the springs and
inflate the sheet. `test_self_collide` gates the sheet's height span against
exactly that.

### Cloth self-collision (sheet piling on the floor)

|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|

| xpbd self-collide gap=0.059988927 | 400 | step | 989314.77 | 0.00 | 395.73 |
| vbd none gap=0.0015972767 | 400 | step | 275444.60 | 0.00 | 110.18 |
| vbd self-collide gap=0.055625502 | 400 | step | 1876406.17 | 0.00 | 750.56 |
| xpbd none gap=0.21782388 | 1200 | step | 764433.67 | 0.00 | 152.89 |
| xpbd self-collide gap=0.21782388 | 1200 | step | 4003866.27 | 0.00 | 800.77 |


## Fluid — lattice Boltzmann (D3Q19)

LBM is the odd one out among this engine's fluid solvers. SPH and PBF carry
particles and spend much of every step deciding which ones interact; LBM carries
a fixed grid and every cell talks to the same 19 neighbours forever. No neighbour
search, no sorting, no pressure Poisson — pressure comes from density by an
equation of state. What it pays instead is memory: 19 floats per cell whether
that cell is doing anything or not, plus a second buffer for streaming.

MLUPS (million lattice updates per second) is the standard figure, so one op
here is one cell advanced one step and ns/op reads as 1000/MLUPS. The scan holds
roughly flat from 16^3 to 64^3, declining slightly as the working set outgrows
cache — which is what a method with no arithmetic intensity to hide behind looks
like when it becomes bandwidth bound.

3.7 MLUPS is defensible for a straightforward scalar implementation and is NOT
close to the best published CPU numbers, which reach tens of MLUPS with SIMD
across cells, a fused collide-stream, and an in-place pattern that drops the
second buffer. None of those are done here. Two things that were done are worth
recording because they were worth 20x between them: the velocity set is
materialised once into the solver rather than rebuilt by a function call 19 times
per cell per step — the first run of this benchmark read 0.18 MLUPS and all of
the difference was allocation — and streaming swaps its buffers instead of
copying one into the other.

Correctness is gated on physics rather than on regression: `test_lbm` checks the
velocity set's moment conditions, reproduces the analytic Poiseuille parabola to
0.28%, and holds a symmetric obstacle in a symmetric tunnel to a y-asymmetry of
6e-8.

The second table is the one that matters: surface force integration by momentum
exchange, which is the step that turns a simulation into a MEASUREMENT. Cd is
compared against the Schiller-Naumann correlation -- an empirical fit to
EXPERIMENT, not to another simulation.

A single resolution agreeing would prove nothing, so the claim is the trend: as
the sphere goes from 3 to 4 to 5 cells in radius the deviation falls 41.5% ->
32.6% -> 26.7%, monotonically, at 4.6x the cost. The remaining gap is what a
staircase-voxelised sphere and a few percent of blockage do to drag, and both
raise it -- which is why the numbers approach the correlation from above.

`test_lbm_force` gates the physics rather than the number: zero force in still
fluid, zero lift on a symmetric body (7e-8 against a drag of 0.17), force growing
between linearly and quadratically with speed as Cd itself falls, and a blockage
of 55% inflating Cd by ten times -- exactly the correction a real wind tunnel
has to apply.

The third table prices the Smagorinsky LES seam (`fluid/lbm.mojo`,
`eddy_viscosity`). It is a seam and not a feature because the constant switches
it off exactly: at `smagorinsky = 0` the collision operator is bit-identical to
plain BGK, which is what `test_lbm_les` gates.

The two cost rows run the same sheared channel for the same number of steps and
change nothing but that constant, so the gap between them IS the per-cell price
of the model — about 29% on the rows below, charged on every cell whether the
flow needs a subgrid closure or not.

The two regime rows are why one would pay it, and they report an OUTCOME rather
than a ratio: a speed ratio between a run that finishes and a run that does not
measures nothing. Past a sphere at nu=8e-4 and u=0.16 — Re~1600 on a 48x24x24
grid, far too coarse to resolve the wake — the unmodelled run leaves the
velocity bounds at step 169, and the same grid at `smagorinsky = 0.17` reaches
step 600. That is the advantage regime stated as a measurement: the model costs
29% everywhere it is not needed, and is the difference between a number and a
divergence where it is.

## Fluids — LBM moving solids and rigid coupling (17.59)

`Lbm` now carries moving solids by Ladd's bounce-back: the reflected
population carries the wall's momentum, and momentum exchange gives each
moving sphere its force and torque. `test_lbm_moving`: Couette flow within
0.003 % of the lid speed, a translating sphere's drag 3 % from
Stokes-Hasimoto, a spinning sphere's torque 4 % from the rotlet.
`physics/fsi.mojo` `LbmCoupler` ties a sphere to a `ContactScene6` body, two
ways, one lattice step per physics step: a 1500 kg/m^3 sphere settles at
0.997 of the Stokes-Hasimoto terminal velocity (`test_fsi`); at density
ratio 0.5 the explicit coupling diverges (added mass).

The first table is the lattice cost per cell with static against moving
solids; moving spheres re-flag their cells every step (`move_sphere` scans
the lattice for the cells a sphere owns, O(cells) per sphere). The second
is one coupled step.

## ECS filters and change detection (17.77)

`World.select(Filter().with_[A]().without[B]())` is evaluated by each
backend natively: the archetype backend tests the filter once per signature,
the bitset backend with word-level AND-NOT, the sparse-set / reactive
backends walk the smallest `with_` store and probe the rest, the naive and
chunked backends scan every id. The comparison column is the pre-17.77 path
(`query1` then `has` on every match, which `Pool.active_query*` used).
`changed[C]` reads a per-component write log covering the last `window`
ticks (8 by default), so it costs O(writes); the comparison is a scan of
every entity for a hand-kept dirty field.

## Foundation — rigid-transform representations (PGA motor vs dual quat vs mat4)

The same random rigid transforms applied and composed through each
representation (see `test_motor_parity` for the equivalence proof).

The second table prices the **Lie layer** (`geometry/galie.mojo`: exp / log /
geodesic on PGA motors) against the classical interpolation routes on the same
transforms. Read it with the semantic caveat: `geodesic3` produces a true SCREW
motion — rotation and translation share an axis and interpolate as one uniform
rigid motion — whereas `slerp + lerp` decouples them and traces a DIFFERENT
path, and a matrix cannot be interpolated at all without decomposing first
(a blend of two rigid matrices leaves SE(3)). So this table prices the
abstraction; it does not claim the routes are interchangeable. The dual-quat
row goes through the motor bridge because `DualQuat` has no native ScLERP —
it prices what the current API makes you pay, not the algorithm's floor.

The chain table is the compact representations' predicted advantage regime.
The per-point APPLY rows above are the matrix's: one dot product per row,
nothing to beat. Composition is the other side — a motor and a dual quat are
8 floats against a matrix's 16, so a long chain streams half the memory.
Sweeping chain length K separates the two regimes instead of letting the
apply rows stand for both.

The gap closes monotonically: at K=4 mat4 leads by ~1.45x, at K=64 by ~1.13x,
and by K=256 the **dual quaternion overtakes it**. The
crossover is real but the margin is small, so the honest reading is that the
8-float representations are competitive on long chains and behind on short
ones — not that they are the better choice outright. The PGA motor stays
slightly behind its dual-quat twin throughout, which is consistent with the
apply rows: same 8 floats, more expensive product.

The next table covers what the CONFORMAL algebra adds over the rigid one:
uniform scale as a versor, and spherical INVERSION. Inversion is the only
transform in the engine with no 4x4-matrix counterpart — it is conformal but
not affine, so it is not a linear map on homogeneous coordinates at all — and
it falls out of the SAME sandwich as plane reflection with a dual sphere
substituted for a dual plane. `test_cga_inversion` gates it against the closed
form and against the structural properties (involution, the sphere fixed
pointwise, inside/outside exchanged) to ~1e-6.

The same table answers the point-to-arc question. A circle IS a first-class
object in this algebra (a grade-2 round, carried here as its sphere/plane
pair), and both scalars the distance needs come back as inner products with no
coordinate case analysis: the signed plane distance is up(p)·π and the radial
term falls out of up(p)·S. But the SPLIT into normal and radial components and
their Pythagorean recombination is the same step the euclidean routine takes —
the algebra replaces two dot products, not the algorithm. Measured at ~6x the
closed form (`test_cga_circle` gates parity to ~5e-6, including a point on the
axis where the closest point is not unique, and a point exactly on the circle
where the subtraction under the root can go negative).

The last table turns the CAPABILITY claim into a measurement instead of an
assertion. A 4x4 matrix cannot absorb an inversion at all, so a matrix
pipeline has to BREAK a mixed chain at every inversion: fold the affine run
into a matrix, transform, apply a closed-form inversion, start a new matrix.
A versor absorbs all of it, so inversion COUNT is the scaling axis.

The shape is exactly what the claim predicts: **the versor is flat in the
inversion count** (~280-290 ns whether the chain holds 0 inversions or 8 —
one folded operator, one pass per point), while the matrix path grows
linearly at ~7.3 ns per inversion (0.97 -> 4.70 -> 23.5 -> 59.6 ns).

It still does not pay. Extrapolating the two lines puts the crossover near
**39 inversions in a single chain**, which no realistic pipeline has. So the
honest summary is that the capability is real and now measured — the versor
genuinely does what the matrix cannot, at a cost that is independent of how
much of it you use — but the 32-component generic multivector makes the
constant so large that the structural advantage never becomes a time
advantage. A specialised conformal versor, in the way `Motor3` specialises
the PGA even subalgebra, is what would move that constant.

One implementation note that the parity suite earned: the rotor's bivector
had a sign error in its y term (mask 4|1 yields the CANONICAL blade e1e3,
which is -e3e1). Every GA-only check passed anyway, because both sides of a
GA-vs-GA comparison carried the same convention. Only the cross-check against
an independent matrix implementation exposed it.

The cost is the story: with versors hoisted out of the loop, the conformal
sandwich runs **~200x** the hand-written closed form for both operations.
So "matrices cannot express inversion" must not be read as "you need CGA for
inversion" — the closed form is three lines and 1.5 ns. What the versor buys
is COMPOSABILITY: scale, rotation, translation and inversion multiply into one
operator that the conformal group is closed under, and `D(a)·D(b) = D(ab)` is
checked as part of the parity suite. That is an expressiveness argument, and
on this evidence it is not a performance one.

## Collision — LBVH build: CPU radix sort vs GPU bitonic sort

An earlier revision of this section reported the device losing at every N and
blamed the bitonic sort's worse complexity. **That conclusion was wrong**, and
the way it was wrong is worth recording: it came from comparing FULL BUILDS,
where both sides share a hierarchy emit that dominates the total, and from two
measurement defects that the shared tail hid.

- The GPU path fed its device-produced Z-order into `build(lbvh=True)`, which
  re-runs the whole host Morton+radix sort. That is idempotent, so the result
  was correct and the row silently charged the GPU path for a CPU sort it
  never needed. `build_lbvh_presorted` now emits from a given order.
- The emit itself recomputed each node's box by rescanning its whole leaf
  range, making it O(n log n) AABB merges for a value the two children
  already hold. Bottom-up bounds made **every** BVH build ~1.7x faster.

With both fixed, and with the sort measured on its own rather than inferred
from totals, the answer reverses: **the device sort wins from ~16k leaves and
by roughly 3x at 65k**, and the full build crosses over too — CPU ahead below
~16k, GPU ahead at 65k. Bitonic's O(n log²n) and its
~136 kernel launches at that size are simply not enough to give the advantage
back.

What stands from the earlier reading is the reason bitonic was chosen at all:
an LSD radix sort must be STABLE, the natural GPU scatter claims slots with a
per-bin atomic in warp-arrival order, and an unstable radix sort is silently
WRONG rather than slow. A stable GPU radix (per-block histograms plus a global
scan) would widen the margin further; it is no longer the difference between
winning and losing.

(no accelerator: CPU rows only)
### LBVH build: CPU radix sort vs GPU bitonic sort

| cpu lbvh (morton+radix, full build) | 1024 | build | 197997.00 | 0.01 | 0.20 |
| cpu lbvh (morton+radix, full build) | 4096 | build | 816998.00 | 0.00 | 0.82 |
| cpu lbvh (morton+radix, full build) | 16384 | build | 4135311.00 | 0.00 | 4.14 |
| cpu lbvh (morton+radix, full build) | 65536 | build | 17160945.00 | 0.00 | 17.16 |
| cpu sort only (morton+radix) | 16384 | sort | 2037989.00 | 0.00 | 2.04 |
| cpu sort only (morton+radix) | 65536 | sort | 8020751.00 | 0.00 | 8.02 |


## Collision — batched GPU raycast vs the CPU BVH

The query-side counterpart to the GPU broadphase, and the one place in this
report where the device wins outright. The BVH answers a ray in O(log n) by
descending only the nodes it enters; the kernel answers it in O(n) by testing
every box, but with one ray per lane. Ray count is the axis, since both sides
amortise a fixed cost over it — a tree build on one side, host<->device
transfer on the other.

**The GPU is 7.5x-9.2x faster at every ray count tested**, including 256 rays.
Parity is exact rather than statistical: same proxy per ray and bit-identical
distances (`test_gpu_raycast` compares proxy ids, because two boxes at nearly
the same distance are exactly where a differing tie-break would hide behind a
distance-only check). Unlike the broadphase kernel this one is fully
deterministic — each thread keeps its nearest hit in registers and writes
once, so there is no atomic and no ordering question.

One caveat that decides how to read the low-ray rows: the CPU row INCLUDES the
BVH build, which is right for a scene rebuilt each frame but wrong for a
static level whose tree is built once and reused. Strip the build and the CPU
cost at 65536 rays falls to roughly 770 ns/ray — still ~6x the GPU, so the win
survives the correction at high ray counts, but the 8x at 256 rays is mostly
the build and would largely disappear against a persistent tree.

(no accelerator: CPU rows only)
### Scene raycast: GPU batched vs CPU BVH (4096 boxes)

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|
| cpu bvh (build+cast) | 256 | ray | 21644.45 | 0.05 | 5.54 |
| cpu bvh (build+cast) | 2048 | ray | 3765.10 | 0.27 | 7.71 |
| cpu bvh (build+cast) | 16384 | ray | 1714.29 | 0.58 | 28.09 |
| cpu bvh (build+cast) | 65536 | ray | 1233.66 | 0.81 | 80.85 |


## Collision — GPU all-pairs broadphase vs the CPU structures

Every CPU broadphase here wins by doing FEWER tests. This one keeps the O(n²)
enumeration and throws lanes at it instead (`collision/bp_gpu.mojo`: one
thread per box walking j > i, hits appended through an atomic cursor). Pair
sets are identical to brute force — `test_gpu_broadphase` gates that on a
sparse and a dense scene, plus an undersized output buffer that must REPORT
overflow rather than truncate silently.

The shape is the result: GPU cost is **nearly flat in N** (~3.4 -> ~5.7 ms
from 512 to 8192 boxes, absorbing 256x the pair tests for 1.7x the time)
while CPU brute force grows quadratically (0.16 -> 43.8 ms). So the device
overtakes every O(n²) CPU path somewhere between 512 and 2048 boxes, and by
8192 it is ~7.6x faster than CPU brute and ~5.7x faster than SAP.

The trend lines cross. The grid is ahead at 8192 (by 1.27x), and by **32768
the device wins ~1.7x** (14.1 ms vs 24.1 ms) — while also emitting 4,892 exact
pairs against the grid's 38,845, an **8x superset**. So above roughly 16k
boxes the GPU is both faster and tighter. One caveat still applies to the
smaller rows: the grid emits a larger, conservative candidate set
(9476 vs 1235 pairs at N=8192), so its row is not equal work — the same
not-apples-to-apples caveat the main broadphase table carries. And the GPU
row is END-TO-END: it includes uploading boxes and reading pairs back, which
is ~3.4 ms of fixed cost that dominates entirely below ~2k boxes. A pipeline
keeping boxes device-resident would pay much less of it.

One structural limitation, stated because it decides where this can be used:
the atomic cursor makes the output ORDER nondeterministic. `solver6` depends
on a deterministic pair order for bit-identical results, so this cannot go
behind that seam without sorting first. It also does not implement the
`BroadPhase` trait at all — the trait has nowhere to carry a device context,
and one context per instance is the pattern that hangs on this nightly.

(no accelerator: CPU rows only)

## Collision — sweep-and-prune vs the other broadphases, by motion speed

SAP (`collision/bp_sap.mojo`) keeps last frame's sorted endpoint order and
repairs it with an insertion sort, so its cost depends on TEMPORAL COHERENCE
rather than on N alone. The sweep axis is therefore how far bodies move per
frame, not how many there are. Pair sets match brute force exactly
(`test_sap`, including across moving frames, a teleport and a population
change), so this table is cost-only.

The scene oscillates with FIXED amplitude, so the overlap count stays ~90-110
pairs at every speed and the rows compare equal work — a sweep that let boxes
drift apart at high speed would be changing density, not coherence.

Three readings, one of them uncomfortable:

- **SAP delivers on its premise**: ~3x cheaper under jitter than under real
  motion, which is the insertion sort finding the order nearly correct.
- **SAP beats the DBVH here at every speed**, by 3-4x. That is not a
  contradiction of the persistent-vs-rebuild table above: the DBVH's advantage
  is bounded by its FAT MARGIN, and that table jitters inside it. Here motion
  is sustained, so leaves escape their fat boxes continuously and the tree
  pays reinsertion every frame. The lesson is that "temporal coherence" is not
  one property: a margin-based structure needs motion below a THRESHOLD, while
  a sort-based one only needs few INVERSIONS, and sustained slow drift
  satisfies the second while violating the first.
- The spatial hash wins outright at every speed ON THIS SCENE, and is
  flat across the coherence axis because it rebuilds from scratch in O(n) and
  has no state to invalidate.

That last row held only because every box in that scene is the same size,
which is the grid's best case. The second table sweeps OBJECT-SIZE SPREAD,
the hash's documented weak point: a hash has one cell size and no single
choice suits a wide spread — big boxes are duplicated into many cells while
small ones leave most cells nearly empty. Two grid rows are run per mix, one
cell sized for the small boxes and one for the large, so the result cannot be
dismissed as a badly tuned grid.

**At a 20:1 spread SAP wins outright**: ~27 ms against ~64 ms for the
best-tuned grid and ~440 ms for the small-tuned one, a 16x gap — and it wins
on the OTHER
axis too, emitting 147,969 exact pairs where that grid emits 732,550, a 5x
superset whose surplus the narrowphase then has to reject. A sorted sweep has
no cell size to get wrong. So the grid is the right default for uniformly
sized worlds and the wrong one as soon as scale varies, which is a sharper
statement than either structure simply being faster.

  [pairs at speed 0.005 ] = 106
  [pairs at speed 0.05 ] = 96
  [pairs at speed 0.25 ] = 83
  [pairs at speed 0.9 ] = 99

## Physics — GPU XPBD cloth (CPU vs GPU scaling)

Gather-Jacobi PBD cloth, identical math on both sides (parity ~1e-6 in
`test_gpu_cloth`). GPU rows only appear on hosts with an accelerator.
(All GPU rollouts share ONE `DeviceContext`: creating several per process
hangs on this nightly — the per-call driver is why this section was empty
in earlier reports.)

The second table splits that end-to-end number into host->device upload,
device compute and device->host readback, and adds the shape a real game loop
has: draining positions back to the CPU EVERY frame. Rows are normalized per
simulation step, so the phases are additive. Read it as the answer to "is the
bus or the kernel the bottleneck?" — and note the answer is workload-dependent,
not a constant. With a single drain, transfers are ~10% of a 65k-particle
rollout: essentially free. Switch to per-frame readback and the bus becomes the
DOMINANT cost — at 65k the readback column alone runs ~2.5x the compute column,
and the rollout total grows ~3x. Against the CPU rows that is the difference
between a ~29x and a ~11x GPU win at 16k particles: **most of the GPU's
advantage on this workload is spent on getting the answer back**, which the
end-to-end rows alone cannot show.

Two second-order effects only the split makes visible: per-frame syncing costs
the compute column itself ~3-6% (it drains the launch pipeline, so kernel
dispatch stops overlapping), and readback cost grows SUBLINEARLY in particle
count while compute amortizes better — so the larger the cloth, the worse
readback looks *relative* to compute (~0.9x of compute at 4k, ~2.5x at 65k).

(no GPU: only CPU rows)
### XPBD cloth: CPU vs GPU (60 steps x 8 Jacobi iters)

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|
| CPU 64x64 (4k particles) | 4096 | step | 1331884.15 | 0.00 | 79.91 |
| CPU 128x128 (16k particles) | 16384 | step | 7540043.75 | 0.00 | 452.40 |


## Geometry — DCGA spike: quartic surfaces as a linear incidence test

The torus was raised as a gap in the CGA coverage. It is not one: a torus is
a QUARTIC, `Cl(4,1)` represents only quadrics and lower, so "torus
intersection via CGA" is a category error rather than a missing feature.
Double CGA (`Cl(8,2)`) is the algebra that does cover it, by tensoring two
conformal copies so products of the two points supply degree-4 monomials.

**The dense algebra is out of reach on this toolchain, and measurably so.**
`Multivector[p,q,r]` unrolls the geometric product fully at compile time, so
cost grows with the SQUARE of the blade count. Measured for one product:
32 blades 4 s, 64 2 s, 128 8 s, 256 30 s, 512 exceeds a 6 GB address-space
cap, and `Cl(8,2)`'s 1024 blades — about a million term products — was killed
by the OOM killer outright. That is a structural wall, not a tuning problem,
and it is the reason the spike is written as the T-space subalgebra instead.

What that subalgebra is: the tensor product of two conformal points spans 15
distinct monomials (s², s·x, s·y, s·z, x², y², z², xy, yz, zx, s, x, y, z, 1
with s = x²+y²+z²), and every Darboux cyclide — planes, spheres, cyclides and
the torus — is a fixed coefficient vector over that basis. So incidence
collapses to a 15-term dot product, and `test_dcga` shows one call answering
all three surface types with nothing changed but the coefficients.

The cost is the notable part. DCGA runs a **flat ~3.87 ns regardless of
surface** — that flatness IS the uniformity — against 2.05-2.36 ns for the
hand-written tests, so **1.6x-1.9x**. Set that against the other GA rows in
this report: CGA sphere narrowphase costs 3.7x its analytic twin, and the
conformal VERSOR paths cost ~200x. The difference is not the algebra, it is
where the algebra is spent — used to DERIVE a representation it is nearly
free, carried as a runtime multivector it is not. The torus row is the
narrowest gap (1.64x) precisely because the hand-written torus needs a square
root and the DCGA form does not.

### DCGA: one uniform incidence test vs hand-written surfaces

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|
| torus  dcga (uniform) | 4096 | test | 10.74 | 93.08 | 0.04 |
| torus  hand-written | 4096 | test | 6.58 | 151.93 | 0.03 |
| sphere dcga (uniform) | 4096 | test | 5.36 | 186.49 | 0.02 |
| sphere hand-written | 4096 | test | 2.84 | 352.16 | 0.01 |
| plane  dcga (uniform) | 4096 | test | 5.39 | 185.67 | 0.02 |
| plane  hand-written | 4096 | test | 2.82 | 354.23 | 0.01 |


## Physics — MLS-MPM (material point method)

MPM sits between the engine's other deformables. FEM keeps a fixed mesh and
therefore a fixed topology; PBF and SPH keep no rest shape at all. MPM carries
elastic state on PARTICLES and uses a background grid, thrown away each step,
only to resolve the coupling — so the material can flow, pile up and merge
while still remembering strain. That is the combination snow, mud and plastic
flow need, and it is the one class of material the other two solvers here
structurally cannot represent.

Plasticity is a determinant clamp on the deformation gradient: strain past a
yield range is FORGOTTEN rather than stored, which is precisely what turns an
elastic solid into a material that keeps a new shape. The benchmark shows it
costs **~0.5%** — it is a clamp and a rescale — and `test_mpm` shows what that
0.5% buys by running the identical dropped column both ways: the elastic body
springs back to height 0.64 after impact, the plastic one stays at 0.28.

Per-particle cost FALLS as the particle count grows (2693 -> 1300 ns) because
the grid is cleared every step regardless of how many particles occupy it, so
a 32³ grid's fixed cost dominates at small counts and amortises away at
larger ones. Read the small-N rows as grid overhead, not as scaling.

### MLS-MPM: particle scaling and the cost of plasticity

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|
| mpm elastic | 96 | particle-step | 8864.52 | 0.11 | 170.20 |
| mpm plastic | 96 | particle-step | 7665.90 | 0.13 | 147.19 |
| mpm elastic | 240 | particle-step | 4166.41 | 0.24 | 199.99 |
| mpm plastic | 240 | particle-step | 4412.06 | 0.23 | 211.78 |
| mpm elastic | 576 | particle-step | 3043.99 | 0.33 | 350.67 |
| mpm plastic | 576 | particle-step | 2711.28 | 0.37 | 312.34 |


## Physics — co-rotational tetrahedral FEM

A continuum next to the engine's mass-spring lattice. `softbody.mojo` joins
particles with distance constraints, so its stiffness and volume response are
properties of how the lattice happens to be wired. FEM discretises the
material instead: each tetrahedron carries a deformation gradient and stress
comes from a constitutive law with real Lame parameters, so those become
material properties. `test_fem` measures the consequence — a pinned
cantilever under gravity holds volume to **0.04%** of rest.

The table prices the CO-ROTATIONAL part specifically, because that is the
trade that decides whether the method is usable. Linear elasticity measures
strain from displacement and is therefore only valid for small DISPLACEMENTS:
rotate an undeformed body and it reports enormous spurious strain. The
`linear (R=I)` rows are that control, run on the same meshes.

Extracting the rotation by polar decomposition costs **~5.6x** (roughly 710 vs
127 ns per tet, flat in mesh size for both since tets are independent). What
it buys is in the error column: after a 63-degree rigid rotation of an
UNDEFORMED body, the linear variant reports a peak spurious force of **83.6**
— which would tear the body apart — while the co-rotational one reports
**0.00**. That is not an accuracy improvement, it is the difference between a
method that works under motion and one that does not, and 5.6x is what it
costs.

### Co-rotational FEM: mesh scaling and the cost of rotation-invariance

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|
| corotational 4x2x2 rot-err=0.00 | 80 | tet | 1222.14 | 0.82 | 0.10 |
| linear (R=I) 4x2x2 rot-err=83.64 | 80 | tet | 189.70 | 5.27 | 0.02 |
| corotational 8x4x4 rot-err=0.00 | 640 | tet | 1262.52 | 0.79 | 0.81 |
| linear (R=I) 8x4x4 rot-err=83.64 | 640 | tet | 189.16 | 5.29 | 0.12 |
| corotational 12x6x6 rot-err=0.00 | 2160 | tet | 1216.32 | 0.82 | 2.63 |
| linear (R=I) 12x6x6 rot-err=83.64 | 2160 | tet | 183.68 | 5.44 | 0.40 |

## Physics — FEM constitutive models (17.58)

`physics/fem_material.mojo` puts the stress law behind a compile-time trait:
corotational (the existing law), stable Neo-Hookean (Smith 2018, without the
log term) and StVK. `test_fem_material` shows they agree within 0.21 % at
strain 1e-3, are all stress-free under a 63-degree rotation, and differ
where they should: an element pushed through itself (det F = -0.2) is
restored only by Neo-Hookean, and StVK's restoring stress at 2 % length is
5 % of its peak. The table is the cost of one force pass per element; the
corotational rows pay the 12 Newton iterations of the polar decomposition.

## Physics — heat conduction (17.61)

`physics/thermal.mojo` adds a temperature field on two discretisations: a
finite-difference grid and the tetrahedra of a `FemBody` (linear elements,
lumped capacity). On a 1D bar with a hot end both stay within 0.03 K of the
erf solution after 10 s and within 0.02 K of each other (`test_thermal`);
`expand` rescales the FEM rest shapes, and a heated free bar lengthens by
alpha_L dT L to 0.0001 %. The table prices one explicit step per node at the
same 1 mm resolution; the stable dt column says how many steps a second of
heat flow costs on each.

## Physics — partitioned coupling schedule (17.61)

`physics/coupler.mojo` steps two solvers through one interface: explicit
staggering, linear interpolation of the partner's state over substeps, or
Aitken-relaxed iteration. The parts here are one-dof masses, so the rows are
the coupler's own overhead per coupled step (interface lists, interpolation,
extra passes), next to the same system written as one loop.

## Physics — SPH vs PBF: explicit pressure against density projection

Both solvers run on the SAME particle state, grid and kernels, so this
isolates the one thing that differs: SPH turns density error into a pressure
FORCE and integrates it explicitly; PBF treats density error as a CONSTRAINT
and projects positions. The metric is cost per SECOND OF SIMULATED TIME, not
per step — per-step cost flatters whichever solver takes smaller steps, and
how large a step each survives is the entire practical question. Each row
carries the settled density and peak speed it reached, so a row that is cheap
because it diverged is marked as such rather than looking like a win.

The timestep story comes out as expected: **SPH is CFL-limited and PBF is
not**. SPH is stable to dt=1/120 and fails at 1/60 (density 1.76x rest, peak
speed 21 vs 0.12); PBF runs stably at 1/30, a 4x larger step.

The COST story does not. Despite needing a 4x smaller timestep, **SPH is
cheaper per simulated second** — ~22 ms against PBF's ~26 ms at its largest
stable step, and ~1.9x cheaper than PBF at matched density accuracy (~22 ms vs
~43 ms). The reason is per-step cost: an SPH step makes two neighbour passes
(density, then forces) while a PBF step makes two PER ITERATION, so four
iterations cost about four times as much and exactly cancel the four-times
larger step.

So the usual framing — PBF is faster because it takes big steps — is not what
this measures. Two honest caveats on how far to carry that. The scene is a
gentle settling column at 216 particles on CPU, and SPH's stable step shrinks
with fluid stiffness and with the velocity field, so a violent or stiffer
scene pushes SPH down while PBF holds — the crossover is a property of how
hard the CFL limit bites, not a constant. And PBF's neighbour lists are built
once per step and reused across iterations here (the standard arrangement);
rebuilding them per iteration, which an earlier revision did, cost ~7% more
for identical settled density.

### Fluids: SPH (explicit pressure) vs PBF (density projection)

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|
| sph dt=1/2000 rho=1.02 v=0.12 | 216 | sim-second | 653622742.00 | 0.00 | 653.62 |
| sph dt=1/500 rho=1.02 v=0.12 | 216 | sim-second | 158476597.00 | 0.00 | 158.48 |
| sph dt=1/120 rho=1.01 v=0.12 | 216 | sim-second | 36622174.00 | 0.00 | 36.62 |
| sph dt=1/60 rho=1.76 v=21.32  UNSTABLE | 216 | sim-second | 14885658.00 | 0.00 | 14.89 |
| pbf dt=1/120 it=4 rho=1.00 v=1.64 | 216 | sim-second | 189300066.00 | 0.00 | 189.30 |
| pbf dt=1/60 it=4 rho=1.02 v=0.70 | 216 | sim-second | 123092288.00 | 0.00 | 123.09 |
| pbf dt=1/30 it=4 rho=1.06 v=0.37 | 216 | sim-second | 65791764.00 | 0.00 | 65.79 |
| pbf dt=1/30 it=8 rho=1.04 v=0.61 | 216 | sim-second | 94972696.00 | 0.00 | 94.97 |

## Physics — Position-Based Fluids

The engine's first fluid, and it is deliberately the SAME solver shape as the
XPBD cloth already here: predict positions, project a constraint, derive
velocity from the position change. Cloth projects a distance constraint, PBF
projects a DENSITY one (C_i = rho_i/rho_0 - 1), so the machinery is shared and
only the constraint changes — which is what made this the cheapest entry into
fluids rather than a parallel body of code.

Rows are read like the cloth ones: cost next to the error it bought, here mean
density relative to rest (1.00 = incompressible). Every iteration count lands
within ~2% of rest and cost is linear in iterations; per-particle cost is
roughly flat as N grows, which is the uniform grid keeping neighbour search
near-linear.

Three things had to be right, and each was caught by a physical gate rather
than by inspection — worth recording because each produced a plausible-looking
fluid that was wrong:

- **Rest density must be CALIBRATED to the particle spacing, not chosen.** With
  rho_0 above what the sampling can reach, every particle reads as under-dense,
  the correction pushes outward everywhere, and the fluid explodes against the
  container. It is now computed from a lattice sum at the seeding spacing.
- **XSPH viscosity must be normalised by density.** The poly6 kernel here is
  unnormalised and of order 1e3, so summing it raw over ~30 neighbours injected
  velocity thousands of times larger than the field it smooths, and the solver
  GAINED energy every step. The dam-break energy gate is what caught it.
- **The per-iteration displacement needs a cap.** Without one the projection
  overshoots far enough to push particles past each other, and behaviour was
  erratic in the ITERATION COUNT — 2 and 4 converged, 3 and 6 collapsed to ~16x
  rest density. Erratic in the iteration count is the signature of divergence,
  not of under-resolution, and `test_pbf` now asserts convergence at every
  count rather than at one.

### Position-Based Fluids: cost at a density-error level

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|
| pbf it=2 rho/rest=0.99 | 216 | particle-step | 4994.69 | 0.20 | 129.46 |
| pbf it=3 rho/rest=0.99 | 216 | particle-step | 3566.70 | 0.28 | 92.45 |
| pbf it=4 rho/rest=0.99 | 216 | particle-step | 5780.25 | 0.17 | 149.82 |
| pbf it=6 rho/rest=0.99 | 216 | particle-step | 7099.89 | 0.14 | 184.03 |
| pbf it=10 rho/rest=1.00 | 216 | particle-step | 15219.82 | 0.07 | 394.50 |
| pbf it=6 rho/rest=1.00 | 512 | particle-step | 15486.09 | 0.06 | 951.47 |
| pbf it=6 rho/rest=1.00 | 1000 | particle-step | 13687.18 | 0.07 | 1642.46 |
| pbf it=6 rho/rest=1.00 | 1728 | particle-step | 20392.40 | 0.05 | 4228.57 |



## Continuum stepping contract (17.55)

FEM, MPM, PBF and LBM (through `physics.continuum.LbmContinuum`) now share
one `ContinuumSolver` interface. `step_checked` adds the failure policy: a
health scan with quarantine before and after each step, counted in
`CONTINUUM_NAN` / `CONTINUUM_ESCAPED`. The table is its cost against a plain
`advance` on the same solver.

## Physics — cloth solver seam (XPBD vs VBD, cost at a quality level)

Vertex block descent (`physics/vbd_cloth.mojo`, arXiv:2403.06321): per-vertex
3x3 Newton steps swept over a checkerboard 2-coloring (edges are bichromatic,
so a color updates in parallel with no atomics — same determinism scheme as the
XPBD gather-Jacobi). Every row carries the worst edge-stretch error it bought,
so cost is read AT a quality level (physical gates + CPU/GPU parity in
`test_vbd_cloth`).

The second table sweeps the TIMESTEP at fixed iterations, which is where
VBD's headline property — an implicit solve that is unconditionally stable —
is supposed to pay off. Stiffness is deliberately not the axis: XPBD's
distance projection is already maximally stiff by construction (it snaps
edges toward rest length), so there is no stiffness knob on that side to turn.

The result does not favour VBD. It never blows up — error stays bounded at
every timestep, which is the stability claim in its weak form — but at a fixed
5 iterations its worst edge stretch grows from ~0% at dt=1/120 to ~48% at
dt=1/8, while XPBD holds ~0% throughout and costs ~3x less per step.

That comparison has a bias which has to be stated: the error metric is worst
EDGE STRETCH, and edge stretch is exactly the quantity XPBD projects onto, so
the metric measures XPBD's own invariant. VBD instead minimises an elastic
energy at finite stiffness, and with the iteration count held fixed it simply
does not converge far enough as dt grows.

The third table removes that bias by measuring the cloth's SHAPE — how far
the sheet has sagged — which neither solver is built to optimise, against
each solver's own 200-iteration answer. It changes the picture. XPBD's
effective stiffness depends on how many projection sweeps it got, so at low
iteration counts its cloth is far too soft: at 10 iterations it has reached
**0%** of its converged shape while VBD is at **34%**, and at 20 it is 30%
against VBD's 61%. **VBD converges 2-3x faster per iteration**, which the
edge-stretch metric could not show at all.

Priced, though, that advantage does not survive: a VBD iteration costs ~3x an
XPBD one, so at matched shape fidelity XPBD is still ~1.7x cheaper (65% of
converged for 694 us against VBD's 61% for 1,169 us). The precise statement
is therefore that **VBD wins the iteration count and loses the iteration
price** — which is a different and more useful claim than having no advantage
regime at all. Worth noting the two converge to nearly the same sag (1.136 vs
1.130), so they do discretise the same material and the comparison is fair.

(no GPU: only CPU rows)

## Mesh cloth (17.56)

`physics.cloth.MeshCloth` runs XPBD on any triangle mesh (stretch per edge,
cross-edge bending, area-weighted masses), collides with the scene's
colliders through the world queries, and takes wind drag per triangle. The
cost per vertex-substep stays flat as the sheet grows (the scene here is a
single box; more colliders mean more candidates per query).

## Physics — point caches (17.63)

`physics/sim_cache.mojo` records per-frame point arrays (cloth, strands,
particles) with an error bound: coordinates are quantised to 2 err,
keyframes store zig-zag varints, delta frames store the integer change, so
there is no drift. Frames with non-finite values and err = 0 are stored raw.
`test_sim_cache`: a 256-point cloth over 240 frames at err = 1 mm is 26.8 %
of raw f32 and every frame is within 1 mm; `CachePlayer` stands in for the
live cloth under `step_checked`. The size floor is one byte per coordinate,
hence the flat bytes-per-point column across error bounds (bit packing
would go below it). The seek table is the trade-off the ROADMAP item asks
to measure: decoding frame 200 from the cache against restoring the
snapshot at frame 192 and re-simulating.

## Differentiable simulation — gradient cost & Field abstraction

The `Field` seam (`RealF`/`DualReal`/`DualBatch`/`RevReal` tape, gradient
correctness in `test_diffsim`/`test_gmv_ad`): what a gradient costs via finite
differences vs forward-mode AD vs the reverse-mode tape, and what the
coefficient-generic `GMV` multivector costs vs the specialized `Motor3`
sandwich. Read the two rollout tables together: on this few-flops-per-step toy
the tape's recording overhead dominates (~19x a plain step), but its cost is
FLAT in the parameter count.

The sweep table MEASURES that claim instead of extrapolating it. Primal work is
held constant (NP x BURST = 2000 steps in every row), so the only variable is
how each method's overhead scales with the parameter count:

- **Reverse tape is flat, confirmed**: NP 8 -> 200 (a 25x range) moves it only
  35.9 -> 39.7 ns/op (+10.6%).
- **Forward differences and DualBatch are linear**, as their rollout counts
  imply (N+1 and ceil(N/4) rollouts); DualBatch holds a constant ~2.86 ns per
  4-lane chunk across the whole sweep.
- **The emitted adjoint changes the picture entirely.** `adjoint s2s` is the
  routine a Taichi / Dr.Jit-class source-to-source tool emits — the step
  function differentiated once and written out as an explicit backward pass,
  with no node list and no indirection. It is **flat in the parameter count**
  like the tape (3.9 -> 4.9 ns/op over a 25x range) and simultaneously **~9x
  faster than the tape**, beating every other method at every NP tested,
  including DualBatch at NP=8 where forward mode should be at its strongest.
- Against the primal rollout (0.87 ns/step) that is **~4.5x**, versus the
  runtime tape's **~40x**. Reverse-mode theory puts the floor near 2-4x the
  primal, so the emitted adjoint lands close to it and the tape's overhead is
  shown to be almost entirely bookkeeping rather than gradient arithmetic.
- One structural correction to the usual claim, which the implementation makes
  unavoidable: source-to-source does NOT eliminate the tape, it shrinks it.
  The ground penalty is a branch on the primal state, so the backward pass
  still has to know which side each step took. But that checkpoint is ONE BIT
  per step instead of a node per operation — roughly two orders of magnitude
  less memory here — which is the real difference, and `test_adjoint` gates
  the replay against both the runtime tape and central differences precisely
  because an off-by-one in that recording would still look plausible.
- **Measured crossovers**: the tape overtakes finite differences at **NP ~ 12-15**
  and overtakes DualBatch at **NP ~ 45-50**. An earlier revision of this report
  extrapolated the DualBatch crossover from two points and put it near ~76; the
  measured value is ~1.5x lower, so that estimate is superseded by these rows.
  Both crossovers move earlier the heavier the per-step primal math.

The last row is the same backward pass GENERATED rather than written out.
`physics/adjoint.mojo` describes one integration step as a small program and
derives its transpose with a `comptime` walk — the transformation Warp and
Taichi perform at JIT time, done at compile time instead, so there is no runtime
generation step and the adjoint is optimised together with the primal.

It lands at 8.5ns against the tape's 50ns and the hand-derived routine's 5.3ns:
5.9x faster than the tape it replaces, and still 1.6x slower than the code a
person wrote. That remaining gap is reported rather than rounded away. Two
things closed most of it and are worth recording: the register file and the
constants moved from heap `List` to stack `InlineArray`, and the opcode
dispatch became `comptime if` so each instruction expands to its own rule
alone. Before both, the generated version ran at 19.7ns — the structure was
already right and the indirection was the whole difference.

The sub-language is restricted on purpose: every operation is linear in the
register file, so the transpose of the step does not depend on where it was
evaluated and the backward sweep needs NO intermediate values — one bit per
step against the tape's one node per operation. `program_is_linear` decides that
at compile time, so a program containing a variable-times-variable product is
reported as non-linear instead of being silently differentiated wrongly.

### Differentiable rollout — gradient cost (2000 steps, 2 params)

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|
| realf baseline (1 rollout) | 2000 | rollout | 1.30 | 767.46 | 0.00 |
| central diff (4 rollouts) | 2000 | grad(2) | 5.10 | 196.23 | 0.01 |
| dualreal (2 rollouts) | 2000 | grad(2) | 4.87 | 205.40 | 0.01 |
| dualbatch (1 rollout) | 2000 | grad(2) | 2.44 | 409.50 | 0.00 |
| reverse tape (1 rollout+sweep) | 2000 | grad(2) | 85.93 | 11.64 | 0.17 |


### Differentiable rollout — parameter-count sweep (NP x BURST = 2000 steps, constant primal work)

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|
| fd forward (9 rollouts) | 2000 | grad(8) | 30.70 | 32.57 | 0.06 |

## Physics — actuators (transmission, force generation, internal dynamics)

`Chain.step` took a torque vector, so every control loop lived outside the
engine and the engine knew nothing about what drove it. An actuator splits
that three ways — where the force lands, how much of it, and what lag sits
between command and force — because each varies independently.

The force law is AFFINE, which is why a motor, a position servo and a
velocity servo are one struct rather than three code paths: they differ only
in which coefficient is non-zero. `test_actuator` checks the position servo
against a hand-written PD loop step for step and finds 6e-8 divergence — the
abstraction is meant to be a repackaging, and a check that it merely
"converges to the target" would pass for any controller at all.

Cost is the first table and it is essentially zero: a bank of position
actuators tracks a bare torque vector within noise at every chain length,
which is the precondition for the abstraction being worth having.

The second table is the regime that justifies internal dynamics. A direct PD
servo must be stable at the caller's timestep, and its stability limit falls
as gain rises. At dt = 1/240 the direct servo holds to kp = 500 and
**DIVERGES at kp = 2000**, while the same law driven through a first-order
actuator stays on target at kp = 8000. That is the trade in one line: the lag
costs response time and buys a usable gain range four times wider.

The filter is also exact in dt — 1 - exp(-t/tau) rather than the naive
`state += (u - state) * dt/tau` — so halving the timestep reproduces the same
trajectory instead of a different one. That error is invisible at any single
dt, which is why the test compares two.

| step + n position actuators | 8 | step | 4908.22 | 0.20 | 1.96 |

| step + n position actuators | 16 | step | 13813.20 | 0.07 | 5.53 |

## Physics — implicit joint properties (ROADMAP 17.51)

`Chain.step_implicit` folds per-joint damping, spring, armature and
Coulomb friction into the step: the same CRBA factorisation as `step`, plus
one matrix-vector product and diagonal terms. Measured 2026-10-13 the two
are within run-to-run noise of each other at N = 8, 32 and 64. What the
implicit form buys is shown in `test_chain_props`: a joint spring of
stiffness 1e5 at dt = 1/60 (omega dt = 10.3) diverges to infinity as an
explicit PD torque and decays from 0.1 rad as the implicit field.

| step_implicit, all properties | 32 | step | 99298.03 | 0.01 | 11.92 |

## Physics — robot sensors (what the controller is allowed to see)

A sensor here is DERIVED from the state the solver already produced rather
than integrated alongside it, so a reading cannot drift away from the physics
that caused it. Two are not simple state lookups and are worth naming: joint
torque is exact inverse dynamics rather than a stored command, and the
accelerometer reads PROPER acceleration.

Proper acceleration is the one with a falsifiable gate. A real accelerometer
reads zero in free fall and +g at rest on a table — not the other way round,
and not -g. The RNEA forward sweep already produces exactly that quantity,
because its gravity trick gives the base -g. `test_sensors` releases a
vertical prismatic joint, which free-falls analytically, and the reading is
**0.0** — a sign error would put 2|g| there, so the test has no tolerance to
hide behind. A second check finite-differences world velocity on a swinging
two-link chain and agrees to **3.6e-4 relative**; that path shares no code
with the sweep, which is what catches the `w x v` term being dropped — a
mistake invisible on a single rotating link.

The seam is how many sweeps a rig costs. Reading each IMU separately is
O(sensors x links) because every call re-runs the forward sweep; sharing one
sweep is O(links + sensors). The first table sweeps both axes. The **N = 1**
rows are the control and they matter: with nothing to amortise the batched
form is slightly SLOWER at every chain length, which is what makes the wide
rows credible. At 24 links with 96 sensors it is **over 60x** faster.

The last block is the alternative anyone reaches for first — step the state,
difference the velocity, subtract gravity. It loses on cost (a second state
evaluation) and, more interestingly, it has a FLOOR: the error falls with h
as first-order theory says, bottoms out near **3.4e-4 relative at h ~ 2.5e-4**,
and then climbs again as f32 cancellation overtakes truncation. No step size
reaches past that floor. The sweep is exact at every step size because it
differences nothing.

## Physics — tendons (one tension, many joints)

A tendon is a length function `L(q)` plus the rule `tau = -F dL/dq`.
Everything else follows from that line, which is also the argument for making
it a primitive: the coupling is stated once in the geometry and the torques
come out consistent, instead of being open-coded per joint by the caller.

Fixed tendons (`L = sum(c_i q_i)`) have the coefficients AS their moment arms,
so they are exact and essentially free — **197-1090x** cheaper than a spatial
tendon over the same joints, which is the reason to keep both kinds rather
than expressing gearing as a degenerate path.

Spatial tendons route through attachment sites and around obstacles. The
wrap is where the interesting claim sits. A tendon over a sphere touches at
tangent points that move with `q`, so `dL/dq` looks like it should pick up
terms through them. It does not: the path is the shortest one on the surface,
so the length is STATIONARY under sliding a tangent point, and those terms
vanish. `test_tendon` checks that against a central difference of the full
length — arc included — with the wrap engaged (path 1.097 vs chord 0.917) and
finds **2.3e-4** agreement. The finite difference knows nothing about the
stationarity argument, so it is a test of the claim and not a restatement.

A second, whole-trajectory gate: the work a constant tension does over 400
simulated steps matches `-F * (L_end - L_start)` to **0.27%**. Path
independence is a property of the whole run, so arms that are right on
average and wrong instant-to-instant do not survive it.

The benchmark's control group is finite-difference moment arms, the thing a
first implementation writes. It is given its best case and it WINS there:
at 1 DOF two length evaluations beat three Jacobian columns per site
(**~2x** faster), and it is still ahead at 2 DOF. The crossover falls
between 2 and 8 DOF; by 24 DOF the analytic arms are **~10x** faster,
because differencing costs an extra path evaluation for every joint.

The accuracy floor is the part that does not move with problem size. The
difference is central, so truncation falls as h² — the sweep shows a clean 4x
per halving — but f32 cancellation takes over below **h ~ 1.6e-2 rad**, where
the best error is **1.1e-5**. The optimal step is nearly a degree of joint
travel and every finer step is worse, which inverts the usual intuition. No
step size reaches the analytic value.

Wrap routing costs an acos-and-rotate per segment whether or not the obstacle
engages; a disengaged obstacle adds **0.2-16%**, most of it at short chains
where the Jacobian work is small enough for the test to show.

## Physics — floating base (six unconstrained degrees of freedom at the root)

`Chain` assumes the root is bolted down, so a reaction wrench of any size is
free and nobody accounts for it. That covers a fixed machine's arm and covers
nothing that moves — no quadruped, no free-flying body, no thrown ragdoll.
Lifting the assumption turns the six root equations into part of the solve.

Three gates carry it. A body with no joints must accelerate at **exactly g**,
which is where applying gravity twice, or in the world frame instead of the
body frame, would show. Pinning the base must reproduce `Chain` EXACTLY —
the pin replaces the six base rows with the identity rather than using a
large-inertia limit, so the comparison can demand machine precision, and it
measures **7.6e-6**. And joint torques, being internal, cannot change total
linear or angular momentum: the falling-cat statement, and the only gate that
exercises the whole (6+n) coupling at once, since a wrong `H_bj` lets a robot
push against itself and drift. Momentum is assembled from per-link world
velocities, sharing no arithmetic with the solve it audits, and the drift is
checked for CONVERGENCE under halving dt so that small means integration
error rather than a tolerance picked to pass.

The first table's control group is the standard workaround: six pseudo-joints
— three prismatic, three revolute — carrying the same base body. **This table
read the other way at first.** With the mass matrix assembled by unit
accelerations the pseudo-joint version won by 2-4.3x. The breakdown table
says why that was worth chasing: assembly was **88-94%** of the solve and the
dense elimination only 5-6%, so the deficit was one routine, not the design.
Extending the composite-inertia recursion to the base made assembly
**7.5-9.8x** faster and reversed the verdict — the quaternion root now wins
at every size, by roughly 1.8x at 2 links narrowing to ~1.2x at 16.

Both assemblies are kept. The unit-acceleration form derives the coupling
block from nothing the recursion shares, so the test demanding the two agree
(**4.8e-7**, coupling block 2.4e-7) is a cross-check rather than a
restatement. That is what a slow second implementation is for.

The last sweep is not a performance result wearing a table. Three revolute
joints are an EULER ANGLE parameterisation and Euler angles are singular: at
90 degrees of pitch the outer and inner axes align and no joint acceleration
produces a rotation about the lost direction. Walking pitch toward it, the
accelerations the pseudo-joint solver must produce to represent one bounded
physical motion run **5.45 -> 2745** while the quaternion base holds 5.45
throughout. Those rows are not slower, they are wrong, and no tuning fixes an
angle that has no inverse.

## Physics — unified constraint solver (equality, limits, dry friction, contact)

Joint limits were resolved in one pass and contacts in another. Every
constraint now goes into one system, solved by projected Gauss-Seidel where
the rows differ only in how their impulse is projected: unbounded for
equality, non-negative for a limit or a contact normal, a box for dry
friction, and a cone for contact friction.

**The convergence argument for doing this did not survive measurement.**
Driven to equal work, alternating passes match one system at every useful
sweep count, and then keep going to machine zero while the unified solver
stalls near 3e-8 — it warm-starts its impulses and recomputes residuals from
a velocity that has had order-1 impulses added and removed, and that
cancellation is the floor. The disjoint control agrees to 5 digits, which is
what says the comparison was measuring coupling and not an artefact.

The real case is that friction COUPLES rows. `|f_t| <= mu f_n` cannot be
evaluated by a pass that does not know `f_n`, so separate passes do not
converge slower to the right answer — they cannot state it.

Which makes the cone the seam worth exposing. A PYRAMIDAL cone bounds each
tangent component separately; its admissible set is a square, and the
consequences are geometry, not tolerance. On the diagonal it permits
**sqrt(2) times** the Coulomb limit — measured at 0.70710677 against an exact
0.70710678 — so sliding friction varies by **41.4%** with the direction of
travel relative to a basis the physics never chose. And once both components
saturate the force points at 45 degrees regardless of where the body is
going: predicted worst misalignment 45 - asin(mu f_n / v) = **35.4 deg**,
measured 35.0 on a 5-degree sweep. An ELLIPTIC cone projects the tangent
pair radially and measures **3.6e-7** spread with **0.0 deg** misalignment.

That correctness costs **under 16%**, most of it at small problems, falling to
3% by 48 coordinates with 16 contacts — the radial projection is a square
root and a scale on two rows that the solver was already touching.

     32       0.0       1.0645344e-07
  disjoint rows (control)
