## Physics — differentiating through RIGID contact

`diffsim` differentiates a soft ground: a spring-damper penalty, smooth
everywhere, where the gradient matches finite differences and everyone goes
home. Real rigid solvers detect a contact and apply an impulse, and that is a
SWITCH. The trajectory is smooth in the initial state only while the sequence
of contacts holds fixed.

The sharp finding is what "fixed" has to mean. It is not the same NUMBER of
bounces — it is the same STEPS at which they fire. `test_diffrigid` shrinks
a probe around a well-posed point and finds that at eps = 2e-3 and 5e-4 the
bounce counts match on both sides while the schedules do not; only at
**1.25e-4** do both match, and there the difference is -0.99993 against a
dual of -1.0. The naive probe — which passes a bounce-count check — returns
**0.328**, a number that is not the derivative of anything. An earlier
revision of this test checked bounce counts and reported that disagreement as
a solver bug.

The boundaries are dense, not pathological: sweeping the drop height over
[0.5, 1.0] changes the contact schedule in **366 of 499** samples.

The probe window narrows as the timestep does — measured **2.5e-3 at
dt = 1/120 down to 1.6e-4** — because the boundaries are spaced by how far
the body travels in one step. Integrating more finely makes the gradient more
accurate and finite differences less able to see it. Forward-mode AD has no
probe and no window.

Substituting the soft model does not rescue this. A critically damped spring
is restitution ZERO, so its gradient decays to 3e-45 while the rigid one is
-1.0 — an O(1) difference that stiffness does not close, because stiffness
was never the parameter that controlled it. That decay also costs: at the
longest rollout the differentiated soft model runs **13x** slower than its
own value-only version against ~1.1x everywhere else, consistent with f32
denormals. A vanishing gradient is expensive as well as uninformative.

|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|

| rigid rollout + forward AD | 180 | rollout | 1043.20 | 0.96 | 0.21 |
| soft rollout (value only) | 180 | rollout | 1012.08 | 0.99 | 0.20 |
| soft rollout + forward AD | 180 | rollout | 1351.07 | 0.74 | 0.27 |
| rigid rollout (value only) | 720 | rollout | 3595.35 | 0.28 | 0.72 |
| rigid rollout + forward AD | 720 | rollout | 4267.89 | 0.23 | 0.85 |

## Physics — the contact solve on the GPU (17.17)

`GpuContactSolver` runs the coloured substep loop of `ContactScene6` on the
device, one thread per contact, calling the same `QuatBody6` methods the CPU
does. The device part is nearly flat in N here — the loop is a few hundred
small kernel launches per frame, so launch count rather than work sets its
cost — and per-frame upload and download of the whole state are of the same
order. Against the CPU's serial solve at the largest N the solve itself is
over an order of magnitude cheaper on the device.

The whole frame is a different story, and the more useful finding: on every
path it is dominated by the CPU-side collection and bookkeeping, which grows
roughly quadratically with N even with the broadphase on (island wake and
sleep loops, and the warm-start cache match — audit F22). That, not the
solve, is what 17.19 has to fix first.

no accelerator: GPU rows skipped

## Animation — graph nodes (17.6)

Blend spaces, masked layers and additive poses on top of the clip runtime.
Cost is linear in bones for every node, and composing nodes adds their costs
— there is no shared setup to amortise. The geodesic (screw) blend costs
about half again as much as the dual-quaternion one; it buys a constant-speed
path, which matters for large-angle blends and not at all for the small ones
most locomotion graphs perform.

## Animation — IK (17.3)

The closed-form two-bone solve and FABRIK put the same ankle on the same
target; the closed form is more than an order of magnitude cheaper and
needs no tolerance, which is why legs and arms use it and FABRIK is kept for
chains longer than two segments (tails, spines, multi-effector bodies).

## Physics — active ragdoll drives (17.2)

A ragdoll that follows an animation adds one soft angular drive per
simulated joint to the joints it already has. The rows price that on top of
the same legs simulated with joints only; it is a fraction of the frame, not
a multiple, because a drive is three scalar rows per joint while the contact
and joint work around it is unchanged.

## Physics — contact rules and breakable joints (17.26 / 17.29)

Per-contact rules (one-way platforms, conveyors, friction overrides) are
applied once per frame to the collected contacts; their scan costs less
than this machine's run-to-run spread, so the rule rows are best read as
"no measurable cost" rather than ordered against each other. Breakable
joints sample every joint's load after each substep while any threshold
is set; that costs a few percent of the frame.

### Contact rules and joint break checks: per-frame cost

## Physics — fields, water, ropes, deformable terrain (17.27 / 17.28 / 17.41 / 17.30)

Region forces are a pass over bodies x fields before the step; sampling the
wind from a grid instead of a constant costs a few times the constant field
and is what lets a simulated flow drive it. Buoyancy's cost is the
submerged-volume estimate: closed form for spheres, midpoint integration
for boxes, where the sample count is the accuracy knob and cubes its cost.
The XPBD rope is several times cheaper than the same span built from rigid
links on distance joints and reaches the same catenary. Terrain edits are
local: re-deriving the vertical bounds from a block summary keeps an edit's
cost independent of terrain size, where a full rescan grows with it.

## Physics — water surfaces (17.60)

`physics/water.mojo` puts the water height behind a `WaterSurface` trait:
flat, Gerstner (closed form in t, so any time can be queried without
stepping) and a shallow-water ripple grid. `float_body` samples a box at n^3
points against whichever surface it is given. `test_water`: zero-amplitude
Gerstner equals flat bit for bit; a box on a 40 m swell heaves with the
wave's period to 0.05 % (5.067 s vs 5.064 s); a ripple on 1 m of water
reaches 5 m in 1.55 s against 5 / sqrt(g H) = 1.60 s. The flat query is a
constant the compiler folds, hence 0 ns. The Gerstner cost is the 4
fixed-point iterations of the inverse map plus one final evaluation, each
over every wave.

`physics/mac_fluid.mojo` is an incompressible MAC grid fluid (semi-Lagrangian
advection, explicit viscosity, PCG pressure projection). On the Taylor-Green
vortex it and the D3Q19 lattice both decay within 0.5 % of exp(-2 nu k^2 t)
(test_taylor_green); per cell-step the lattice is cheaper, and the MAC
cost grows with resolution because the PCG iteration count does.

## Physics — hair strands and Hill muscles (17.57)

`physics/strand.mojo` simulates strands by Follow-The-Leader (Müller 2012)
with a straight-rest bending pull: segment lengths stay at rest to 1e-6
without colliders (`test_strand`), a ponytail swings 39 cm after a 90-degree
head turn and settles within 2.3 mm. The first table is the cost per particle
per step, without colliders and with a head sphere plus two shoulder capsules.
The second prices the Hill curves added to `Actuator.muscle`
(`HillParams`: force-length, force-velocity, passive); with all three
switched off the muscle is bit-identical to the plain one (`test_muscle_hill`).

## Physics — joint kinds, limits and motors (ROADMAP 17.47)

A 64-link chain hanging under gravity with one sideways push, joined by one
kind throughout. A ball chain runs no ext pass at all (`any_ext` stays
False, the pre-17.47 path); the limited kinds pay for the ext rows on every
joint, measured 2026-10-13 at +34 to +54 % per step at iters 4 and +65 to
+85 % at iters 8 -- the slider adds two linear and three angular equality
rows to its travel limit, the cone-twist a swing row to its twist limit.

## Physics — LOD, budget and deterministic replay (17.31 / 17.39)

The budget controller trades iterations for time; the curve below says how
much that costs in quality on a settled stack, and in this range the answer
is almost nothing -- the stack's residual compression is set by the soft
contacts' compliance, not by the iteration count. Freezing bodies by
distance removes their work outright. Replay seeks by restoring the nearest
snapshot and re-running the inputs after it, which lands on the same state
as a full re-run bit for bit; the snapshot interval sets the seek latency.

## Gameplay — save games vs snapshots (17.40)

A snapshot keeps everything the solver needs to continue bit-exactly; a
save game keeps what the game chooses and survives code changes. For the
same crates the save is an order of magnitude smaller and several times
faster to write and to read back, because it carries poses and velocities
only and lets the level script rebuild the rest.

### Save game (schema, bodies only) vs full snapshot: write + read

| snapshot read | 64 | scene | 813586.00 | 0.00 | 0.81 |
| save write (5103 B) | 64 | scene | 31862.00 | 0.03 | 0.03 |
| save read + apply (level rebuild excluded) | 64 | scene | 26377.00 | 0.04 | 0.03 |
| snapshot write (1026112 B) | 1024 | scene | 6672447.00 | 0.00 | 6.67 |

| save read + apply (level rebuild excluded) | 1024 | scene | 1967977.00 | 0.00 | 1.97 |


## Batched articulations and narrowphase (17.17)

Both GPU kernels are written as a per-thread body on flat arrays that the host
can also run, so the host rows below are the kernel's own arithmetic. The
articulation body (ABA, no allocation, revolute and prismatic joints) beats the
existing per-chain `Chain.step_aba`, which allocates its sweeps' Lists on
every call; the narrowphase body calls the same manifold functions as
`pair_manifold`, so its host cost matches. Device rows (fused steps with
resident state; narrowphase including transfers) appear only on a host with an
accelerator — this report was generated on one without, so they are absent.

## Fluids — LBM on the GPU, interpolated walls, a second force path (17.42)

The same D3Q19 stencil runs on the GPU from the CPU grid's setup and agrees
with it to float32 noise. Throughput grows with grid size as launch
overhead amortises, but the largest grid here is still far below what the
memory bandwidth allows, so these rows are a first port, not a tuned
kernel. On the CPU the interpolated bounce-back costs no measurable extra
per step; the control-surface force is a single pass over a box of cells.

## ECS — reflection-driven serialisation (17.11)

`ecs.schema` builds a type's field table from the compiler's own reflection
(`reflect[T]`): dotted names, type names, offsets, sizes, no hand-written
list. A record written with it can be read by a later version of the type,
matched by field name. That self-description is what the rows price. Written
one value at a time, each record carries every field's name and type, and
costs tens of times what a hand-written fixed-order writer does; the batch
form writes the descriptors once and matches names once, which removes most
of the gap. The hand-written row also stores less — it skips the cached world
matrix the schema faithfully includes.

### Transform serialisation: reflection-driven schema vs hand-written

| schema batch read | 16 | value | 424.50 | 2.36 | 0.01 |

| hand-written read | 16 | value | 20.81 | 48.05 | 0.00 |

## Physics — the contact solver, differentiable and batched (17.20 / 17.18)

`physics/diffsolver.mojo` re-states `ContactScene6`'s per-frame algorithm —
speculative collection with the warm-start cache rule, then per substep
gravity, warm start, the soft normal + Coulomb friction sweeps, pose
integration and the relax sweep — for dynamic spheres on static planes, over
any `SolverField` scalar. Every branch is written with an indicator
(`positive`), so the same code carries a forward or reverse derivative, or
steps one world per SIMD lane. Against `ContactScene6` on the same scenes the
`RealF` instance agrees to float32 noise (`test_diffsolver`), and the forward,
four-lane forward, reverse and finite-difference gradients agree.

**Layout.** One `RealF` world per step costs the same as eight worlds packed
into the lanes of `BatchReal[8]`, so the batch runs roughly **7x** more
world-steps per second, and fanning the batches over cores adds another ~3x
at this machine's core count. The N = 1 control is slightly slower batched,
as it should be: one world pays for eight lanes. Lane k is not bit-identical
to scalar world k after contact (~1e-6 over 60 frames): the SIMD and scalar
lowerings contract multiply-adds differently.

**Gradient.** `DualBatch` (four directions per rollout) is the cheapest
gradient here at every NP measured. Reverse mode is flat in NP by
construction, but its tape costs several rollouts' worth up front — the
ratio to central differences falls from about 6x at NP = 1 to about 2x at
NP = 16 — so the crossover lies beyond the NP this row sweeps (every sphere
pair is carried every frame, O(NP^2), which also makes large NP expensive
for everyone).

## Physics — reduced-coordinate contact (articulated bodies that can touch)

Until this landed the two halves of the engine could not meet: `Chain`
integrates in joint space, `ContactScene6` resolves contacts in maximal
coordinates, and nothing connected them — an articulated body could not touch
anything. The bridge is the point Jacobian, which maps joint velocities to one
material point's velocity and whose transpose maps an impulse there back to
joint torques, so a contact is solved without leaving generalised coordinates.

Cost scales with the ARTICULATED SYSTEM, not with the contact pair: every
impulse needs `J H⁻¹ Jᵀ`, so a Jacobian, the CRBA mass matrix and a dense
solve. That is the axis these rows sweep. The contact pass runs ~1.7-14x the
bare dynamics step depending on link count, most expensive at SHORT chains
where the step itself is nearly free and the fixed per-contact work dominates.

Two implementation notes the measurement forced. H depends only on q, which
does not change during a velocity iteration, so hoisting it out of the
contact loop cut the 2-link case by 1.7x — it had been recomputing an O(n³)
solve per contact per iteration. What remains is one dense solve per impulse;
factorising H once and reusing the factorisation is the next step and is not
done here.

And the contact is SPLIT into a velocity pass and a positional one. Folding
the penetration correction into the velocity target as a Baumgarte bias is
the obvious formulation and it pumps energy — the positional term is not a
physical impulse, so what it adds to the velocity stays there and returns as
speed on the next bounce. Measured at nearly 3x the free-swinging peak before
the split, and below it after (`test_chain_contact` gates exactly that).

| step + ground contact (H factored once per pass, 17.51) | 2 | step | 19505.13 | 0.05 | 3.90 |
| step only | 4 | step | 2222.79 | 0.45 | 0.44 |
| step + ground contact (dense solve per impulse) | 4 | step | 17506.20 | 0.06 | 3.50 |
| step + ground contact (H factored once per pass, 17.51) | 4 | step | 16304.73 | 0.06 | 3.26 |
| step only | 8 | step | 5281.61 | 0.19 | 1.06 |

## Physics — articulated chain: reduced (CRBA+RNEA) vs maximal coordinates

The same n-link pendulum chain in both formulations (physics parity via the
shared analytic period, test_chain): what exact reduced coordinates cost vs
soft maximal-coordinate joints as the chain grows.

The second table separates INVERSE from forward dynamics. Forward dynamics
forms the CRBA mass matrix and solves it densely; inverse dynamics recovers
the torques with one O(n) Newton-Euler sweep and no matrix at all. Both go
through the same `_rnea` routine — with zero acceleration it is the bias
term forward dynamics subtracts, with a real one it IS inverse dynamics —
which is what makes `test_inverse_dynamics`'s round trip meaningful: tau ->
qdd -> tau agrees to ~1e-6 across chains, a branching tree and a forest.

The gap widens with link count exactly as the complexity difference predicts:
2.3x at 2 links, 3.0x at 8, 5.2x at 24, **17.6x at 64**. That ratio is why a
controller computes feed-forward torque by inverse dynamics rather than by
solving forward dynamics backwards.

The joint-kind table exists to show a design claim holds: a prismatic joint
differs from a revolute one ONLY in its motion subspace — S is (axis, 0) for
one and (0, axis) for the other — so every sweep picks a half and nothing
else changes. A mixed chain therefore tracks a revolute one, which it does
(slightly faster, since a prismatic joint has no rotation to compose).

The limit rows price the one-sided constraint pass at ~1-3% while nothing is
against a stop, because the pass counts violations first and returns
immediately when there are none. Limits are solved as impulses rather than by
clamping q: clamping leaves the velocity pointing into the stop, so the joint
re-violates every step and jitters, and it moves the joint without a
corresponding impulse, which injects energy. `test_joints_lib` gates the
difference by requiring the joint to STAY on the stop under a driving torque
for 960 steps, not merely to be inside range once.

### Inverse vs forward dynamics (O(n) sweep vs dense O(n^3) solve)

|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|
| inverse dynamics (RNEA) | 2 | step | 759.27 | 1.32 | 0.46 |
| forward dynamics (CRBA+solve) | 2 | step | 1392.80 | 0.72 | 0.84 |
| inverse dynamics (RNEA) | 8 | step | 1846.74 | 0.54 | 1.11 |
| forward dynamics (CRBA+solve) | 8 | step | 5501.07 | 0.18 | 3.30 |
| inverse dynamics (RNEA) | 24 | step | 4059.13 | 0.25 | 2.44 |
| forward dynamics (CRBA+solve) | 24 | step | 45673.99 | 0.02 | 27.40 |
| inverse dynamics (RNEA) | 64 | step | 9178.33 | 0.11 | 5.51 |
| forward dynamics (CRBA+solve) | 64 | step | 392331.02 | 0.00 | 235.40 |

## Physics — island-parallel solver (serial vs worker threads)

Bit-identical by construction (islands share nothing, test_islands_par);
the honesty row shows the thread overhead when there is one big island.

The second table is the **core-scaling (Amdahl) curve**: the same scene with
the fan-out width pinned to 1..20 workers, min-of-3 per point. Worker count
changes only the schedule — `test_islands_par` gates that every width is
bit-identical to serial — so the curve compares one computation at different
widths, not different computations.

It does NOT scale linearly, and that is the useful result: throughput
saturates near **1.6x** and stops improving past ~4 workers on a 20-thread
host. Inverting Amdahl at that plateau puts the parallel fraction of
`step_soft` at only **~40%** — the other ~60% (broadphase, island labelling,
pose integration, sleep update, contact-cache management) is still serial, and
that serial remainder, not the thread pool, is what caps the solver. It is
also the concrete reason the scheduler seam only repays its threads at larger
N: there is simply not much parallel region to amortize them over.

Two further readings. `workers=1` is a few percent SLOWER than the serial
path here — the pure fan-out tax with no parallelism to win (on the finer
grained colored curve below the same tax falls inside run-to-run noise).
Note also that above ~4 workers the curve is FLAT, so which exact width comes
out best moves between runs (typically 6-12); read the plateau, not the
argmax. And the dip at `workers=8`
reproduces under min-of-3 in BOTH this curve and the colored one below, two
workloads with completely different task granularity, which makes it a
property of the host rather than of either workload. The likely mechanism
(untested here) is topology: this is a 6 P-core / 8 E-core part, so a width
of 8 has to place workers on E-cores or across hyperthread siblings while a
barrier waits on the slowest of them, whereas 12 maps onto the P-cores'
hyperthread set.

### island-parallel solver: serial vs threads

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|
| serial 16 towers x4 | 64 | step | 418025.90 | 0.00 | 125.41 |
| parallel 16 towers x4 | 64 | step | 322268.40 | 0.00 | 96.68 |
| serial 1 tower x8 | 8 | step | 54662.91 | 0.02 | 16.40 |
| parallel 1 tower x8 | 8 | step | 68290.22 | 0.01 | 20.49 |


### island-parallel core scaling: 16 islands at pinned worker counts

## Physics — within-island parallelism (graph coloring vs plain Gauss-Seidel)

One box pyramid is ONE island, so island-parallelism buys nothing there;
coloring parallelises *inside* it. `test_colored` proves the colored schedule
is deterministic and thread-count-invariant; the small-island row is the
honesty row showing what the schedule + fan-out overhead costs.

Its core-scaling curve is the FINE-GRAINED counterpart to the island one
above: there a task is a whole island, here it is a single contact pair
inside a color, and the fan-out is re-entered once per color per iteration.
Despite that structural difference the two curves land on the same ceiling
(~1.5-1.6x) and share the same dip at 8 — which is what makes
the ceiling look like a property of the host and the serial remainder rather
than of task granularity. Finer tasks do cost something visible though: the
colored path needs more workers before it beats its own serial baseline.

## Physics — solver pair collection (brute O(n²) vs the full `BroadPhase` seam)

`_collect_pairs` double-looped every body pair; `broadphase=True` now swaps
that for `self.bp` — ANY `BroadPhase` backend `ContactScene6[B, BP]` is
instantiated with (ROADMAP 17.0e moved the collider registry and the
candidate-pair narrowphase out to `collision.collider_set`/
`collision.contact_gen`, so this is the path the engine actually runs, not a
throwaway per-step BVH). Bit-identical by construction regardless of backend
— `collect_bp_pairs` canonicalises every pair to `a < b` and sorts ascending
before the narrowphase ever sees it, proven for brute/BVH/DBVH/SAP/hash
grid/octree in `test_solver_bp_seam` (six backends × box-stack, mixed
trimesh+hull+sphere+capsule, and four extreme cases). Table 1: brute wins at
small N (no build/query overhead), BVH wins once the O(n²) enumeration
dominates. Table 2 sweeps N = 64..4096 across every backend — the regime
where DBVH's persistent, frame-coherent structure should start winning over
BVH's rebuild-every-step one (advantage-regime rule: most towers do not move
between frames, so DBVH pays near-zero per step once settled). Table 3 is
SAP/hash-grid's own advantage-regime scene: uniform body sizes vs a 20:1
spread, where SAP's single sweep axis and a hash grid's fixed cell size are
each a plausible place for the O(n) claim to quietly stop holding.

### Parallel narrowphase and contact prep (17.54)

`SolverConfig.parallel_collision` runs `try_pair` over fixed chunks of the
candidate list and `make_contact` per raw contact through `cfg.dispatch`,
joining the results in candidate order, so a parallel step is bit-identical
to a serial one (`test_collide_par`). It pays where the narrowphase is
expensive (bodies on a heightfield test a few dozen triangles each) and not
on box piles, where the narrowphase is ~3 % of the step and the serial BVH
broadphase is a larger share. Below 256 candidate pairs it stays serial.

#### Workers sweep (us per step)

| scene | N | contacts | serial us | par w1 | par w2 | par w4 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| box piles | 64 | 64 | 2027 | 2051 | 2005 | 2141 |
| box piles | 1024 | 1024 | 33571 | 27120 | 30294 | 31807 |
| box piles | 4096 | 4096 | 131614 | 116631 | 118940 | 111358 |
| hulls/spheres/boxes on a heightfield | 64 | 160 | 2255 | 2249 | 2147 | 2267 |
| hulls/spheres/boxes on a heightfield | 1024 | 2893 | 55225 | 51212 | 43405 | 44260 |
| hulls/spheres/boxes on a heightfield | 4096 | 15248 | 381593 | 379881 | 291285 | 279050 |

#### Large N and CCD (us per step)

| case | N | contacts | serial us | par w4 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| box piles (broadphase query in parallel above 1024 bodies) | 100000 | 100000 | 3193722 | 3284094 |
| heightfield, every body falling at 30 m/s, CCD on (clamp fractions in parallel) | 4096 | 14480 | 2729588 | 1503071 |

## Physics — kinematic motion type (ROADMAP 17.24): cost of the path

`MOTION_KINEMATIC` is a third instance of the same "advance this body's pose"
morphism `MOTION_STATIC`/`MOTION_DYNAMIC` already were: infinite mass for
impulses (never written by `apply_impulse`), but its velocity is still READ
every contact point (`BodySet.moves`), because that is what carries a
platform's motion into the dynamic boxes riding it. A static body's velocity
is never read at all. This table pins N=16..1024 dynamic boxes on one wide
platform and times a full `step_soft` with the platform STATIC vs KINEMATIC
(velocity held constant) — the delta is exactly that one extra
`velocity_at` read per contact point, at solver scale; `physics.material
.combine`'s per-pair dispatch (next section) runs identically either way, so
it does not show up in this delta.

Every box is pinned awake (`set_can_sleep(id, False)`) in BOTH columns —
without that, the STATIC column's boxes settle and sleep within a few dozen
frames (`test_sleep6`'s own number is 35), and `_impulse_inert` then skips
their pair OUTRIGHT every subsequent step. That sleep/skip delta dwarfs one
extra `velocity_at` read and would swamp the thing this table exists to
isolate; an earlier revision of this bench measured exactly that confound
(kinematic 2–3x SLOWER, because its always-moving platform never lets its
boxes sleep at all) before the pin was added. With both columns forced
fully active every step, the kinematic/static ratio lands at 0.97x–1.00x
across N=16..1024 (the `flock`-locked recorded run below) — inside
run-to-run noise, not a real cost.

### Kinematic path cost: N boxes on a static vs kinematic platform

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|
| static platform | 16 | step | 448641.52 | 0.00 | 53.84 |
| kinematic platform | 16 | step | 429329.36 | 0.00 | 51.52 |
| static platform | 64 | step | 1352916.84 | 0.00 | 162.35 |
| kinematic platform | 64 | step | 1335449.58 | 0.00 | 160.25 |
| static platform | 256 | step | 6857276.25 | 0.00 | 822.87 |
| kinematic platform | 256 | step | 5990162.54 | 0.00 | 718.82 |
| static platform | 1024 | step | 41660063.28 | 0.00 | 4999.21 |
| kinematic platform | 1024 | step | 42716864.04 | 0.00 | 5126.02 |


## Physics — sleep API wake storm (ROADMAP 17.25)

`wake(id)` (and the `_wake_island` it shares with `teleport` and with
`_refresh_islands`'s own automatic wake) scans every body in the scene once
to find the ones sharing `id`'s island label — O(bodies), not O(island
size), by construction: it reuses the same flat index space
`_refresh_islands` already walks every step. This table builds a single
N-body island (island size == scene size, the worst case for that scan),
settles it to sleep, then wakes ONE body in it repeatedly at increasing N —
a synthetic "wake storm" stress on the scan itself, isolated from any actual
physics cost.

The scan is O(bodies), not O(1) and not O(island size only) — the numbers
confirm it: roughly 37/128/440/2308 ns per `wake()` call at N=16/64/256/
1024, a ~3.4–5.2x cost increase for each ~4x step in N, tracking N roughly
linearly (each point is already an average over 3000 repeated calls, so the
larger jump at the top end is read as per-call measurement noise, not a
super-linear scan). A production scene with one sleeping body per island and a small
number of islands pays a few hundred ns per wake regardless of total scene
size (the scan only walks the CALLER's scene, not "every scene"), but a
single scene with many thousands of bodies and frequent manual wakes (a
large destructible level, say) would want a per-island member list instead
of this flat scan — not needed yet at the sizes ROADMAP 17.1/17.2 exercise,
noted here as the honest scaling ceiling.

### Sleep API: wake(1 body) cost vs island/scene size

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|
| wake 1 of N | 16 | wake | 44.95 | 22.25 | 0.13 |
| wake 1 of N | 64 | wake | 121.51 | 8.23 | 0.36 |
| wake 1 of N | 256 | wake | 643.90 | 1.55 | 1.93 |
| wake 1 of N | 1024 | wake | 2226.84 | 0.45 | 6.68 |


## Physics — materials: per-pair combine-mode dispatch cost (ROADMAP 17.23)

Four combine modes (`COMBINE_AVERAGE`/`MIN`/`MULTIPLY`/`MAX`), one pair-rule
function (`physics.material.combine`, PhysX's "the higher-priority mode of
the two bodies wins"), called ONCE per contact PAIR per substep (not once
per contact point — `_solve_pair` computes `pair_mu` before its point loop).
This table runs the exact same N=1024-contact scene with all four modes set
uniformly, to check the claim that choosing a mode changes only which branch
`combine` takes, not the solver's asymptotic cost.

All four modes land in the same 14.1–15.6 M ns/step band (~1.1x spread) —
within the run-to-run noise this kind of whole-step timing carries at
N=1024, not a mode-dependent cost. The claim holds: `combine` is a 4-way
`if`/`elif` over comptime `Int` constants, and which branch it lands on
doesn't move the needle at solver scale.

### Materials: combine-mode dispatch cost, N=1024 contacts

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|

| MIN | 1024 | step | 14558385.37 | 0.00 | 873.50 |
| MULTIPLY | 1024 | step | 12876827.62 | 0.00 | 772.61 |
| MAX | 1024 | step | 13466900.32 | 0.00 | 808.01 |


## Physics — per-body parameters (ROADMAP 17.48)

N free-falling spheres (no contacts), 20 steps. `default` never calls a 17.48
setter (`any_props` False: the pre/post passes are skipped and the step is
bit-identical to before, which the golden gate checks); the other rows turn
the passes on for every body. Measured 2026-10-13: every variant within
±5 % of `default` at N = 1000 and 10000, i.e. inside run-to-run noise --
the step is broadphase and integration, the two O(N) passes are not visible.
The first version of `add_force` woke the island through `wake_island`,
an O(N) scan per call, so a force on every body every step was O(N²): 2.0x
the step at N = 10000 (4106 vs 2073 ms). It now resets the sleep timer
directly when the body is awake (islands sleep together) and only scans for
a sleeping body; the same row measured 2308 vs 2371 ms after the change.

## Physics — step-impulse report (ROADMAP 17.49)

The contact events' impulse payload, `contacts_of`, `net_contact_force` and
`joint_reaction` read per-step impulse totals the solver accumulates after
every substep (`IMPULSE_SUBSTEP_SUM`, default) or after the last one, scaled
by the substep count (`IMPULSE_LAST_SUBSTEP`). The accumulation always runs.
Measured 2026-10-13 on N resting boxes: the rows below differ by less than
the run-to-run spread, and the interleaved A/B at N = 1024 puts the three
extra accumulation passes of the sum estimator at a ratio of 1.003, not
resolved (per-round 0.92 - 1.12). `test_impact_report` shows why the sum is
the default: on a 5 m/s impact it reads 5.1633 N s (m v + m g dt exactly),
the last-substep estimate 0.1633.

## Collision — BVH build heuristic (median-split vs binned SAH)

`test_sah` proves the two builds answer every query identically. Binned SAH
cuts a TIGHTER tree (lower Σ node area: 0.67x of median on the clustered
scene, 0.89x on the uniform one) and that shows up in cheaper raycasts —
1.35x on clustered, 1.14x on uniform, the smaller gain being the honest
result when a uniform scene leaves the two trees near-identical.

LBVH is the third row family: sort leaves by Morton code, split at the
highest differing bit. Its split decisions fall out of the sort instead of
being searched for, which is what makes it the standard construction to
parallelise. Here it **builds ~10x faster than median and ~6x faster than
SAH**, and its tree lands BETWEEN the two on tightness — so it dominates the
median build outright (cheaper to build, no slower to query) and trades
against SAH on a clean crossover: SAH repays its pricier build after roughly
**40k rays** on these scenes. Below that ratio — a tree thrown away every
frame, which is exactly the broadphase case — LBVH is the right choice; above
it, SAH is. Parity for all three builds is in `test_lbvh` (including
duplicate Morton codes, where no bit differs and the descent must fall back
to a median split, and a flat sheet with zero extent on one axis).

The textbook framing of this trade is "SAH buys query speed with a slower
build", and that is NOT what these rows say: SAH also **builds ~1.7x faster**
than median-split here. That is a property of this implementation, not of the
heuristic — the median path sorts centroids with an insertion sort (O(n²)),
while binned SAH only histograms into 12 bins per axis (O(n)). So the honest
reading is that SAH dominates on both axes *because the median baseline's
build is the weak one*; a median split over a proper O(n log n) sort would
close the build gap and restore the classical trade-off.

  [clustered] Σarea median 977014.0  SAH 651650.1875  LBVH 701393.25  (SAH/median 0.6669814224770576 , LBVH/median 0.7178947793992717 )
  [clustered] SAH repays its build vs median after ~ 311 rays
  [clustered] SAH repays its build vs LBVH after ~ 57194 rays
  [clustered] LBVH DOMINATES median (cheaper build and no slower)
  [uniform] Σarea median 1279621.0  SAH 1132919.625  LBVH 1236453.875  (SAH/median 0.8853556052925046 , LBVH/median 0.9662656950769016 )
  [uniform] SAH repays its build vs median after ~ 374 rays
  [uniform] LBVH DOMINATES SAH (cheaper build and no slower)
  [uniform] LBVH DOMINATES median (cheaper build and no slower)

| clustered raycast median | 4000 | ray | 539.45 | 1.85 | 2.16 |

## ECS — chunked (paged) columns vs one growable column

`ChunkedBackend` is `NaiveBackend` with exactly one thing changed: a column
is a list of fixed 1024-row pages instead of a single growable `List`.
Dense id indexing, the Optional cell, the generational lifecycle and the query
shape are all identical, so the naive-vs-chunked pair isolates the storage
layout and nothing else. Both run the full parity suite
(`test_backend_parity`, `test_iter_parity`).

The result is negative for chunking on every axis measured: ~2x slower to
grow, ~1.8x slower to iterate, ~2.5x slower under churn. The structural claim
does hold — page count is bounded by N/1024 by construction (118 pages for
60k entities across two columns) and no existing row is ever copied — but it
does not convert into time here, for two reasons worth separating:

- The baseline it is supposed to beat is already good. A growable `List` grows
  amortised O(1) and its realloc is a `memcpy`, which is fast enough that
  avoiding the copy saves less than the page indirection costs.
- Every access pays `pages[id/rows][id%rows]`, and unlike the copy, that cost
  is paid on the hot path rather than amortised over growth.

The advantage regime was then sought rather than assumed, and it exists — on
the MEMORY axis, not the time one. A page can only be handed back once its
whole id range is dead, so the shape of the deaths decides everything:

- **spread death** (every tenth id survives): 90% of entities gone and **not
  one page** released, because every page still holds a live entity. Chunking
  returns nothing.
- **region death** (the low 90% of the id range dies together — a level
  unloaded, a wave cleared, anything that allocated its ids in one run):
  **89% of the footprint comes back** (51,200 cells held against a growable
  column's 480,000, which never shrinks at all).

So the regime is not "many entities died" but "a REGION of the id space
died", and the price is ~1.6-1.9x on the shrink and regrow operations
themselves. `test_backend_parity` gates the release against the failure that
would make the memory number meaningless: neighbouring pages' entities must
be untouched, and a released page must be reusable.

The honest scope of the TIME result: the commercial chunk stores this
mirrors (Unity DOTS, Unreal MassEntity) do not use chunks primarily to avoid
reallocation. They use them because a chunk is an ARCHETYPE-HOMOGENEOUS block,
which makes it a natural unit for SIMD iteration and for handing one chunk per
worker to a job scheduler. This variant reproduces the allocation property but
not the layout property — it pages a dense id-indexed column, not an archetype
block — so it measures the weaker half of the idea. A chunked ARCHETYPE
backend, where a page is a homogeneous component block, remains the version
that could plausibly win, and is not what these rows rule out.

### Chunked (paged) columns vs one growable column

## Concurrency — work stealing vs static fan-out

`parallelize` fixes the split before any task runs, so a round costs as much
as the unluckiest block. `scheduler/workstealing.mojo` starts from the same
split and lets idle workers drain other ranges; a claim is one atomic
`fetch_add`, so every index is still issued exactly once no matter how the
steals interleave (`test_workstealing` gates that, including workers > tasks).
Its cursors are spaced one per cache line — packing them would give back more
than the stealing wins, as the false-sharing table below shows.

The result only means something against a cost distribution, so three are run:

- **uniform** — static partitioning is already optimal, so this prices what
  stealing's atomics COST when there is nothing to rebalance. At 2-4 workers
  it is a dead heat, which is the answer: the mechanism is close to free.
- **skewed** (cost rises with index) — stealing wins **~1.5-2x**, the margin
  growing with worker count because a wider static split gives the unlucky
  worker a proportionally larger share of the tail.
- **spiky** (a few very expensive tasks) — mostly a wash. With 512 tasks and
  16 spikes, static blocks each catch roughly the same number of spikes by
  luck, so there is little imbalance left to fix. Stealing pays off when the
  cost gradient is systematic, not merely when some tasks are expensive.

One result worth separating out: on the UNIFORM workload stealing pulls ahead
again at 8-12 workers. Nothing about the tasks is uneven there — the CORES
are. This is a 6 P-core / 8 E-core part, so once the width forces work onto
E-cores an equal split of tasks is no longer an equal split of wall time, and
the dynamic pool absorbs that while a static one cannot. That is the same
topology effect the scaling curves show as a dip at 8 workers, seen from the
other side.

## Concurrency — false sharing (cache-line contention)

The core-scaling curves showed the solver saturating near 1.5-1.6x and Amdahl
explained most of it. This table answers the rest: is the parallel region
itself paying for coherence traffic? Every row does identical work and yields
identical totals (verified at the end of the run) — only the ADDRESS SPACING
of the per-worker accumulators changes.

With atomic increments the effect is unambiguous: packing accumulators onto
one 64-byte line costs up to **~11x**, and the penalty GROWS with worker count
(~3.5x at 2 workers, ~11x at 12) because every added core joins the ownership
ping-pong. Spacing them one per line removes it completely, and stride 16 buys
nothing over stride 8 — confirming this is a cache-line property, not a
"wider is always better" gradient. Stride 2 is the instructive middle: it is
as bad as stride 1 while the workers still fit on one line, then halves once
it splits them across two.

The `plain` rows are a deliberate NULL RESULT and are the reason this
benchmark uses atomics at all. A non-atomic `p[i] += v` loop shows NO stride
effect whatsoever and runs ~30x faster than the atomic version, because the
compiler keeps the accumulator in a register and writes back once — collapsing
thousands of contended stores into a single one. It measures the optimizer,
not the hardware. `local` (accumulate in a local, store once) costs the same
as `plain`, which confirms the diagnosis and is also the pattern that makes
kernels immune by construction.

Bearing on this engine: the solver's parallel paths write whole bodies and
contact pairs at island/color granularity, not packed per-worker counters, so
there is no such accumulator array to contend over. Taken with the Amdahl
reading above, the 1.5-1.6x ceiling is the SERIAL REMAINDER, not contention —
which is what makes the serial 60% the right thing to attack next.

### false sharing: per-worker accumulators at increasing address stride

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|
| atomic  w=2 stride=1 | 2 | incr | 31.56 | 31.69 | 0.63 |
| atomic  w=2 stride=2 | 2 | incr | 30.51 | 32.78 | 0.61 |
| atomic  w=2 stride=8 (1/line) | 2 | incr | 8.31 | 120.35 | 0.17 |
| atomic  w=2 stride=16 | 2 | incr | 8.21 | 121.87 | 0.16 |
| plain   w=2 stride=1 | 2 | incr | 1.04 | 959.69 | 0.02 |
| plain   w=2 stride=8 (1/line) | 2 | incr | 1.04 | 958.73 | 0.02 |
| local   w=2 (store once) | 2 | incr | 1.00 | 999.00 | 0.02 |

| atomic  w=4 stride=2 | 4 | incr | 72.03 | 13.88 | 1.44 |

| atomic  w=4 stride=16 | 4 | incr | 8.29 | 120.61 | 0.17 |

## Procedural — scatter, Delaunay, terrain (17.69)

The noise functions finally have consumers. `procedural/scatter.mojo`
(Poisson-disk, dart throwing, jittered grid; hashed randomness, so a seed
reproduces the points bit for bit), `procedural/delaunay.mojo` (Bowyer-Watson
on the exact `orient2d` / `incircle`: cocircular lattices triangulate
correctly) and `procedural/terrain_gen.mojo` (fBm / ridged / domain-warped
heights as a pure function of world position, so tiles agree on their seams
bit for bit; thermal erosion). `test_pcg`: Poisson minimum distance 2.00002
at r = 2; Delaunay of 400 points gives 784 = 2n - 2 - h triangles; a box
comes to rest on a generated `HeightField`. The Delaunay rows grow per point
with n: the cavity search is linear in the triangles (a point-location walk
is the known next step).

## `diag` — observability layer (Phase 17.9 / 17.10 / 17.32-17.34)

`diag` is layer 0 (`docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` §1): zero engine dependencies, so
every other package can use it, and it carries the detect/record/terminate
half of the error-handling policy (§2). Four tables:

Table 1 (log call cost) is the zero-cost claim for a disabled log level: a
call to `log[level]` where `level` is above the compiled-in `LOG_LEVEL`
compiles its body away entirely (`comptime if`), so it should cost the same
as not calling it at all. The "enabled level" row is the real, non-zero cost
of actually building and pushing a `LogRecord` — included so the "disabled is
free" claim isn't just "everything here is free".

Table 2 is the `FrameArena` vs `List`-per-frame seam (`docs/CATEGORY.md`
§2.2): one allocation, bump-reset every frame, vs a fresh `List` built every
frame, across an N sweep from 64 to 65536 records. `FrameArena` holds flat
around ~1.9 ns/record regardless of N (it never allocates after
construction); `List`-per-frame starts well behind at small N (allocation
overhead against few elements to amortize it over) and closes some of the gap
as N grows, but never catches up in this sweep — allocation dominates at
every size tested here, which is the regime the task asked this table to
cover.

Table 3 is `DrawQueue[dtype]` push+tick throughput — six command kinds behind
one flat tagged struct (not a `Variant`, see `diag/draw.mojo`), pushed and
aged every frame.

Table 4 is trace span overhead, traced vs untraced, wrapping a
solver-phase-sized workload (thousands of ops per span, not one op per span —
a span is meant to wrap a whole solver phase, not a single scalar op; wrapping
a single op in ANY profiler makes its fixed cost look enormous and would
misrepresent how spans are actually used here). Built without
`-D LUDENS_TRACE` (the default, matching `pixi run benchmark`), tracing is
compiled to a no-op and the two rows land within noise of each other. Rebuilt
with `-D LUDENS_TRACE` (`mojo run -D LUDENS_TRACE -I build
benchmarks/bench_diag.mojo`, not part of the default report generation since
one compiled binary only sees one value of the switch), the traced row still
lands within noise of the untraced row at this granularity — the ~50ns fixed
cost of one span's open/close (String-carrying `SpanEvent`, two `List`
push/pop) is a fraction of a percent of a 20,000-op phase, which is the "a
few % or less" bar this table exists to check.

| disabled level (TRACE) | 200000 | call | 0.00 | 6250000.00 | 0.00 |
| enabled level (ERROR) | 200000 | call | 12.50 | 80.01 | 2.50 |

## Physics capture (17.80)

`physics.capture.Capture.record` appends one frame per step (every body's
pose, velocities, sleep flag and island; every contact's normal, points,
depths and impulses) and a full snapshot every `key_every` frames;
`CaptureReader.frame` decodes any frame without simulating. Frames are not
delta-encoded yet, so size grows with the body count; a region keeps only
the bodies inside it.

## Operations boundary — outbox, achievements, black box (17.73)

`diag/outbox.mojo` is the one way operations data leaves the simulation: a
bounded queue of typed numeric records, written during a tick and drained by
the host after it, with drop-newest + `OUTBOX_DROPPED`, deterministic
sampling and rollback deduplication. `gameplay/achievements.mojo` (unlock
once, state in flags), `gameplay/loc_keys.mojo` (stable FNV key ids, missing
key report) and `gameplay/blackbox.mojo` (log + counters + snapshot + input
tail in one checksummed blob) sit on top. `test_ops`: a scene with telemetry
on is bit-identical to one with it off; a black box restored in a clean
scene replays to the failing tick bit for bit. The second table is the
overhead per step; the first version re-grew the drained list from empty
every tick and cost 16 % of the step (2.8 us per record).

## Splines — parametric curves and rotation-minimising frames (17.36)

`geometry/spline.mojo` unifies generalized Catmull-Rom (uniform / centripetal
/ chordal, selected by a comptime `alpha`) and piecewise cubic Bezier behind
one evaluator: a Catmull-Rom segment converts to its exact equivalent
`CubicBezier` (Hermite-to-Bezier on the Barry-Goldman value+tangent), which is
what the arc-length table, `closest_point`, and RMF-frame machinery are
written against. That conversion is the `docs/CATEGORY.md` §2 seam ("same
curve, two representations") — checked by `test_spline_parity`: the uniform
case equals the textbook closed-form Bezier control points
`P1, P1+(P2-P0)/6, P2-(P3-P1)/6, P2` to within 1e-6.

Table 1 is `eval()` cost per family (path = 63 segments / 64 control points).
`CubicBezier` (one segment, no segment lookup) is the floor. `BezierPath` — a
`CatmullRom` converted ONCE via `to_bezier_path()` — costs ~1.8x that (one
segment-locate plus the same Bernstein evaluation). Calling `.eval()` directly
on a `CatmullRom` object costs substantially more, because it rebuilds that
one segment's equivalent Bezier from scratch every call rather than caching
it: ~4x a `BezierPath` eval for uniform (alpha=0, no transcendental call —
its knot deltas are always exactly 1), and an order of magnitude more for
centripetal/chordal, which both call `pow()` three times per rebuild
(centripetal ~18x, chordal ~10x a `BezierPath` eval). Centripetal
consistently lands ~1.8x above chordal across repeated runs despite both
calling `pow()` the same number of times — plausibly this libm fast-paths an
exponent of exactly 1.0 (chordal) but not a fractional one (0.5, centripetal).
Practical takeaway: a caller evaluating the same Catmull-Rom curve
repeatedly should call `to_bezier_path()` once and evaluate that, not
`CatmullRom.eval` in a loop — exactly what `build_arc_length_table` and
`build_rmf_frames` do internally.

Table 2 is the arc-length table: build cost grows with the sample count N (as
expected — it is an N-sample scan), while a single `sample_at_distance` query
against an already-built N=256 table costs ~150x LESS than building that same
table from scratch — the intended usage is build once per curve, query many
times per frame (`examples/15_spline_rmf.mojo`'s constant-speed stepping does
exactly this, one query per step, never rebuilding).

Table 3 is `closest_point` (16 coarse samples + up to 8 Newton refinement
steps, each a combined `eval_deriv`) on the same 63-segment path — ~60x a
single `BezierPath` eval, consistent with doing on the order of 16-24
evaluations per query before it converges.

### spline eval: ns/sample per family (N_ctrl=64)

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|
| CubicBezier (1 segment) | 4096 | eval | 3.27 | 305.38 | 0.01 |
| CatmullRom uniform (a=0) | 4096 | eval | 26.07 | 38.35 | 0.11 |
| CatmullRom centripetal (a=0.5) | 4096 | eval | 92.48 | 10.81 | 0.38 |
| CatmullRom chordal (a=1) | 4096 | eval | 52.03 | 19.22 | 0.21 |
| BezierPath (pre-converted) | 4096 | eval | 5.83 | 171.60 | 0.02 |

| closest_point (16 coarse + 8 Newton) | 1024 | query | 365.95 | 2.73 | 0.37 |



## Scheduler — deterministic timers (17.35): binary heap vs hierarchical wheel

`TimerQueue` is the seam: `TimerHeap` (array binary min-heap keyed by
`(due_tick, schedule sequence)`) against `TimerWheel` (a two-level ring --
an exact-slot near ring for the next 256 ticks, a coarse far ring cascading
into it every 256 ticks). Both are gated bit-for-bit identical by
`test_timers`' seeded randomized schedule/cancel/advance script; this table
is cost-only.

Table 1 is `schedule()` alone (O(log N) heap push vs O(1) wheel bucket
append). The two cross between N=1000 and N=10000: at N=1000 the heap is
still faster (~44 ns/op vs ~57 ns/op -- the wheel's two 256-slot ring
allocations are a fixed cost that small N has not amortized yet); by
N=10000 the wheel is ahead (~55 ns/op vs ~71 ns/op) and stays ahead through
N=1000000 (~28 ns/op vs ~47 ns/op).

Table 2 is the regime the design note calls out: N timers scattered within
a 256-tick window (near-due, matching the wheel's near-ring size), drained
by one `advance()` call. Here the wheel wins across the WHOLE tested range,
not just past a crossover -- even at N=100 (~80 ns/op vs ~134 ns/op) -- and
the gap widens with N: at N=1000000 the heap costs ~324 ns/op against the
wheel's ~43 ns/op, roughly 7.5x (the widest gap in this table, at N=100000,
is ~8.2x). This is the "wheel's advantage regime is large N with near-due
timers" the design note predicted, and it is a wider win than the raw
schedule-only table shows, because it also captures the heap's O(log N)
POP cost (paid N times) against the wheel's O(1) bucket scan.

Table 3 holds N fixed at 200000 and instead sweeps how far out the timers
are scattered: near (256 ticks, inside the near ring), medium (20000 ticks,
forces the far ring and a cascade), far (300000 ticks, past the two-level
horizon documented in `scheduler/timers.mojo` -- timers alias into a far-ring
slot and get re-filed, still not due, every time that slot's 65536-tick
cycle comes back around). The heap's cost is flat regardless of window
(~200-211 ns/op throughout -- it never cared where timers were due, only how
many are live); the wheel's rises from ~22 ns/op (near, ~9x ahead of the
heap) to ~58 ns/op (medium, ~3.6x ahead) to ~135 ns/op (far, ~1.6x ahead) --
still ahead of the heap at every window tested here, but converging hard,
which is the honest cost of the tradeoff the module docstring discloses
rather than a free lunch.

|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|

| wheel | 100 | schedule | 122.82 | 8.14 | 0.01 |

| wheel | 1000 | schedule | 66.76 | 14.98 | 0.07 |
| heap | 10000 | schedule | 60.97 | 16.40 | 0.61 |
| wheel | 10000 | schedule | 79.01 | 12.66 | 0.79 |
| heap | 100000 | schedule | 56.77 | 17.61 | 5.68 |
| wheel | 100000 | schedule | 44.00 | 22.73 | 4.40 |
| heap | 1000000 | schedule | 49.86 | 20.06 | 49.86 |
| wheel | 1000000 | schedule | 48.64 | 20.56 | 48.64 |

## Procedural — tweens: easing dispatch cost and interpolant seam (17.35)

`ease[kind]` (`kind` a comptime bracket parameter) against `ease_dyn(kind)`
(`kind` a runtime `Int`, walking a `comptime for`-generated if-chain) for
the SAME easing, so table 1's delta is dispatch cost alone. Comptime
dispatch really is free: it compiles straight to the one formula, no branch
at all -- `EASE_QUAD_IN` costs ~1.2 ns/op comptime. Runtime dispatch costs
more for BOTH kinds tested, and the extra cost does not grow with chain
position the way a hand-rolled linear if-chain would suggest: `EASE_QUAD_IN`
(id 1, near the front of the 31-entry chain) pays ~11 ns/op more than its
own comptime row, and `EASE_BOUNCE_INOUT` (id 30, the LAST comparison) pays
~10 ns/op more than ITS comptime row -- essentially the same dispatch
overhead whether the chain has to walk past one comparison or thirty, which
reads as the compiler lowering the dense 0..30 equality chain into a jump
table rather than a linear scan. Either way, only the comptime path is
actually free; `ease_dyn` is for the case that needs it (a
data-driven/loaded-at-runtime easing choice), not a substitute for it.

Table 2 is `Tween[T].value()` per tween per frame, N = 1e3..1e5, across the
three interpolants `procedural/tween.mojo` ships: `lerp_real` and
`lerp_vec3` both settle at ~2.2-2.3 ns/op once N is large enough to amortize
the `List[Tween[...]]` backing store's own allocation (the `vec3` N=1000 row
reads high, ~27 ns/op, purely from that one-time setup cost, not from
interpolation), and `geodesic3` holds flat at ~95-98 ns/op regardless of N --
on the order of 40x the lerp interpolants' steady-state cost (the precise
multiplier is noisy at the lerp side, since ~2.3 ns/op is close to this
harness's own per-iteration timing resolution). That gap is exactly the
price of what a `Motor3` tween buys over lerp+slerp: two motor `log`/`exp`
per call (`geometry/galie.mojo`) instead of one FMA, in exchange for
constant angular+linear speed along the true screw path
(`docs/CATEGORY.md` §3) -- pay it for rigid motion, not for scalars or plain
vectors.

### Easing dispatch -- comptime vs runtime (N=2e6 calls)

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|
| quad_in | 2000000 | comptime | 1.62 | 615.78 | 3.25 |
| quad_in | 2000000 | runtime | 5.25 | 190.40 | 10.50 |
| bounce_inout | 2000000 | comptime | 4.02 | 248.83 | 8.04 |
| bounce_inout | 2000000 | runtime | 7.90 | 126.66 | 15.79 |

## ECS — entity pools: acquire/release vs spawn/despawn churn (17.37)

High-churn gameplay entities (projectiles, effect proxies, AI waves) pay full
`spawn` + N component `set`s + `despawn` on every cycle; a `Pool` pre-spawns
its slots once and cycles them between "acquired" and "released" (a
`Disabled[DID]` marker component, toggled through the SAME `set`/`remove`
every other component already uses — zero changes to `StorageBackend` or any
of the six backends) without ever despawning. `acquire` re-applies the
template on every call (not just once at prime time, since a released slot
still holds whatever values its previous occupant left it at), so the
comparison is apples to apples: both sides write the same three components
every cycle. The difference is HOW those writes land — a fresh `spawn` is
adding each component for the first time (an archetype relocation per call on
`ArchetypeBackend`: 0 → {A} → {A,B} → {A,B,C}), while a pool slot already
carries every template component permanently, so the identical `set` calls
overwrite each column in place (`ArchetypeBackend.set`'s "already present"
branch) — plus `despawn`'s full teardown vs `release`'s one-component
`Disabled` toggle.

The table's N is the churn REPEAT count, not a live-entity count (each cycle
nets back to zero net entities), and BOTH variants build a fresh `World`
inside the timed closure — the pool variant additionally pays its one-time
`Pool.prime` there too, which is what makes the **N=1 control** mean anything
(docs/design/wave-a-services.md §17.37 requires it): with a fresh `World` +
one `prime` call charged to a single acquire/release, the pool row must read
slower than a bare spawn/despawn, or the wide rows proving a win at scale
would be measuring noise instead. It holds on all six backends — pool is
1.19x-1.74x SLOWER at N=1 — then crosses over by N=1000 and stays faster
through N=100,000, once `prime`'s fixed cost is amortized across enough
cycles to be outweighed by the avoided relocations:

| backend | N=1 (pool/spawn) | N=100,000 churn speedup (pool vs spawn/despawn) |
|---|---:|---:|
| sparse | 1.38x slower | 1.51x faster |
| archetype | 1.49x slower | 1.28x faster |
| bitset | 1.52x slower | 1.08x faster |
| reactive | 1.45x slower | 1.29x faster |
| naive | 1.74x slower | 1.42x faster |
| chunked | 1.19x slower | 1.19x faster |

`ArchetypeBackend` shows the largest N=1 penalty (a fresh `World` there also
builds the empty archetype-0 table) and a large sustained win (relocation is
the most expensive thing the churn avoids); `BitsetBackend` shows the
smallest win at scale, because a presence-bitset write is already nearly as
cheap as an overwrite-in-place, so there is less relocation cost to avoid in
the first place — consistent with `ecs/pool.mojo`'s own claim that the win is
specifically "no archetype migration of the template", not a universal
constant-factor speedup.

## Scheduler — gameplay event bus (17.38): pull vs push delivery

`EventChannel` is the seam: `Channel[E]` (pull -- a shared double buffer plus
one cursor per reader, the production implementation) against `PushChannel[E]`
(fan-out -- a copy into every subscriber's own inbox at `send()` time, the
`scheduler/message.mojo` mailbox style, kept only as the seam's comparison
partner). Both are gated bit-for-bit identical by `test_events.mojo`'s parity
and determinism checks; these tables are cost-only. ns/op here is per
(reader, event) DELIVERY -- one reader observing one event -- so pull and push
are compared on the same unit even though the work happens at different
times (pull pays at `read()`, push pays at `send()`).

Table 1 fixes 1000 events/frame and sweeps the READER COUNT, 1..32. This is
where the seam's two cost models actually diverge, and there is a real
crossover: at 1 reader push is ahead (~3.3 ns/op vs pull's ~5.3 ns/op --
pull's per-frame bookkeeping, e.g. `update()` scanning `prev` against every
cursor, has more fixed overhead to amortize at the smallest reader count);
by 2 readers the two are within noise of each other (~4.6 ns/op each); from 4
readers on, pull is ahead and stays ahead, widening as readers grow — 8
readers ~3.75 vs ~5.15 ns/op (push ~1.4x costlier), 16 readers ~3.57 vs
~11.66 ns/op (~3.3x), 32 readers ~3.47 vs ~12.31 ns/op (~3.5x). Pull's ns/op
actually drifts DOWN slightly as readers grow (more reads amortize the
per-frame fixed cost over more deliveries); push's rises past what a pure
"O(readers) copies, so ns/op should be flat" model predicts, which reads as
fan-out to many SEPARATE per-subscriber `List`s costing more than the copies
alone once there are enough of them to hurt cache locality — a real cost the
design note's "push costs copies ∝ readers" framing doesn't separate out,
worth flagging rather than folding into "copies" silently.

Table 2 holds the reader count fixed at 4 (inside the crossover band table 1
found) and sweeps events/frame, 1e2..1e6: pull and push stay close across
the whole range, neither consistently ahead by a wide margin (both land
5-7 ns/op through 1e5; at 1e6 push edges ahead, ~4.9 vs ~6.8 ns/op) --
consistent with table 1's story that the seam's real divergence is driven by
READER COUNT, not by how many events pass through per frame.

| pull | 1 | delivery | 4.24 | 235.87 | 0.04 |
| push | 1 | delivery | 6.25 | 160.07 | 0.06 |
| pull | 2 | delivery | 2.93 | 341.49 | 0.06 |
| push | 2 | delivery | 25.03 | 39.96 | 0.50 |
| pull | 4 | delivery | 2.34 | 427.54 | 0.09 |

| push | 8 | delivery | 6.02 | 166.03 | 0.48 |
| pull | 16 | delivery | 1.61 | 622.01 | 0.26 |
| push | 16 | delivery | 11.78 | 84.87 | 1.89 |
| pull | 32 | delivery | 1.92 | 520.12 | 0.62 |
| push | 32 | delivery | 13.00 | 76.92 | 4.16 |

## World queries on real geometry (17.13)

`collision.world_query` answers rays, sphere and capsule sweeps, overlaps and
penetrations against the actual collider surfaces (the older `SceneQuery`
rows above test against broadphase boxes). Sweeps use conservative
advancement over signed distances, so a capsule sweep costs several distance
evaluations per candidate, each a short golden-section search along the
segment. At small N the capsule sweep is the expensive row; by a few hundred
colliders every row is dominated by the per-query linear work (building every
body's pose and scanning world AABBs), which is the step the persistent
broadphase is meant to take over.

## Collision — query prefilter, batches, ray traversal (ROADMAP 17.53)

`QueryIndex` is the frame's poses plus a BVH over every collider's world box,
built once and shared by many queries; its candidate lists are sorted by
collider index, so `ray_cast_indexed` returns exactly what the linear scan
does (`test_query_ext`: 400 / 400 rays bit-identical). Measured 2026-10-13
with the build counted in its row: 3.6x faster at N = 16, 18x at N = 4096
(93.6 vs 5.2 us per ray). The batch rows show the limit of the parallel
dispatch here: the index build (3.4 ms for 4096 colliders) is serial, and
the remaining ~2 us per ray did not get faster on this 4-core container
even with 64-ray items, so the batch is serial-equivalent at this size.
Mesh rays now descend the triangle BVH and heightfield rays walk the grid
(DDA): a 1 km diagonal ray over a 1024 x 1024 field tests 88 triangles,
where the segment's bounding box held 1,457,456.

| QueryIndex (incl. build) | 256 | ray | 904.41 | 1.11 | 0.93 |
| linear scan | 1024 | ray | 32524.07 | 0.03 | 33.30 |

## Shape kinds (17.52)

Cost per resting body-step of the shape kinds added in 17.52 against the
analytic sphere and box. Ellipsoids meet every other shape through GJK
distance and EPA on their exact support mapping (one contact point per
pair); a cylinder is a 16-segment hull whose side contact against a box is
the capsule manifold; a box on an SDF samples 26 points against the grid.
The second table is the error a sampled hull carries and the support
mapping removes.

## Compound shapes (17.44)

One body with N box children against N box bodies held together by
N - 1 weld joints (the 17.5 route for multi-part rigid objects). The
compound has no joint rows and no joint residual; the welded bar's ends
drift apart by the weld error.

## CCD shape coverage (17.46)

Hull bullets at 60 m/s (1 m per step, 20x the wall's thickness) against a
thin wall, and the same hulls at rest as a control. Box / sphere / capsule
pairs keep the closed-form swept box; pairs with a hull, ellipsoid,
compound or static mesh use conservative advancement on the exact convex
distance, with candidates from a BVH over swept boxes.

## Contact model variants (17.50)

Pyramid vs elliptic friction cone, and the elliptic cone with static
friction and rolling / spin resistance on every body. On the slope
diagonal the pyramid caps friction at sqrt(2) mu N, so its cubes slide
about half as far (analytic 0.650 m vs 1.177 m in 1 s); the elliptic runs
match the analytic slide on any direction. Timings compare different
motions (the elliptic cubes travel further), so they are an upper bound on
the cone's own cost; the rolling / spin branch adds two small angular solves
per pair.

### Contact model variants (17.50): cost per step

| N | scene | pyramid us | elliptic us | elliptic + static + rolling/spin us | slide / roll (m): pyramid, elliptic, full |
|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|---|

| 16 | balls rolling at 2 m/s | 126 | 119 | 187 | 1.9999834 , 1.9999834 , 1.8594146 |
| 256 | cubes on a 30 deg slope (diagonal) | 8043 | 7132 | 9593 | 0.6523283 , 1.1983181 , 1.1778312 |
| 256 | balls rolling at 2 m/s | 2547 | 2887 | 3418 | 1.9999833 , 1.9999833 , 1.8594117 |

## Character controller (17.1)

One `update` of the kinematic capsule controller: a few capsule sweeps for
move-and-slide (and a step attempt when a low obstacle blocks it), a
penetration check and a ground probe, all through the world queries above.
Its cost therefore follows theirs: a few microseconds on an empty floor,
then growing with the obstacle count through the queries' linear prefilter.

| update | 16 | step | 2479.01 | 0.40 | 0.59 |
| update | 64 | step | 4878.80 | 0.20 | 1.17 |
| update | 256 | step | 14674.02 | 0.07 | 3.52 |



## Movement modes (17.67)

`gameplay.movement.Mover` adds modes (walk / fall / swim / fly), jump feel
and external velocity on top of the controller, with three velocity models
(direct, acceleration, Quake-style). The model only changes the horizontal
integration, so the cost is the controller's sweeps either way.

### Movement modes (17.67): one update

| model | mode | ns per update |
|---|---|---:|
| direct | walk | 838 |
| acceleration | walk | 830 |
| quake | walk | 838 |
| acceleration | swim | 2948 |

## Gameplay — dynamic character body (17.66)

`gameplay/phys_character.mojo` `DynamicCharacter` is a capsule inside the
solver held at a ride height by a ground spring-damper along a ray, steered
by a capped horizontal force. On flat ground it stops in the same distance
as the kinematic `Mover` (0.58333 m both, `test_phys_character`); unlike it,
an impulse moves it by J / m and its ground spring loads the body it stands
on. One finding on the way: the solver applies accumulated force once per
step and gravity every substep, so a resting body's end-of-step velocity
reads -0.061 m/s; damping that reading held the capsule 6.5 mm high, so the
damper uses the height change per tick. `gameplay/balance.mojo` has the
centre of mass, support polygon, capture point and margin. Rows: cost per
character per tick.

### Character backends, 64 walkers (17.66)

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|
| kinematic Mover.update | 64 | character-tick | 2409.30 | 0.42 | 18.50 |
| DynamicCharacter.update + step_soft share | 64 | character-tick | 4455.55 | 0.22 | 34.22 |



## Sequences and camera rig (17.68)

A `procedural.sequence.Sequence` is evaluated as a pure function of time
(binary search per track); a `SeqCursor` walks from the last segment for
playback. Both give the same values bit for bit; the cursor only pays off
when tracks have many keys. The spring arm's cost is one sphere cast.

## Combat: melee detection and projectiles (17.71)

Three ways to find what a swinging blade touched in one tick: the new pose
only (discrete), the blade's end points on straight lines (linear), the
blade along the real arc (arc). Below a target's thickness per tick they
agree; above it the discrete test misses most thin targets and the linear
one misses those the chord of the arc passes inside. Projectiles cast one
segment ray each per step.

| 0.3 | 18 | 26 | 100 | 100 | 86 | 467 | 2042 |
| 0.6 | 36 | 14 | 97 | 100 | 91 | 682 | 3800 |
| 1.2 | 72 | 5 | 87 | 100 | 99 | 1123 | 6863 |

## Gameplay — rules framework (17.70)

`gameplay/rules.mojo`: attributes with modifier stacks in fixed point
(1/1000 units in integers, so sums commute exactly), hierarchical tags as
bitsets, effects (instant / duration / infinite / periodic, refresh or
count stacking, granted tags, immunity), a damage pipeline (resistance,
shield, health, one death report) and byte serialisation. `test_rules`: the
three evaluators agree on all 4002 reads of a seeded 20000-step sequence;
an actor saved mid-effect continues to the same bytes. Rows: cost per
attribute read. The incremental evaluator keeps running sums but still
scans the modifier list for overrides on every read, which is why it gains
less than the dirty cache.

## Gameplay — flow, objectives, inventory (17.72)

`gameplay/flow.mojo` (state stack deciding what ticks; typed world flags
with change events and by-name serialisation), `gameplay/objectives.mojo`
(event-driven objectives with prerequisites; cycles refused at `add`) and
`gameplay/inventory.mojo` (stacked slots; seeded weighted loot).
`test_flow`: linear and indexed dispatch complete the same 300 objectives
in the same order over 5000 events; 100000 loot draws land at
70.011 / 25.002 / 4.987 % for weights 70 / 25 / 5. Rows: cost per event.
The first version snapshotted every objective's activeness per event
(O(N)), which capped the index at 2x; snapshotting only the listeners
gives the ratio below.

## Raycast vehicles (17.4)

One `Vehicle.update` per car per step: four wheel casts (the seam: one ray, a
swept sphere or a swept capsule per wheel), the suspension, and the implicit
per-axis tire solve (bisection on the rising branch of the Pacejka-shaped curve,
the part that lets a parked car hold a slope). On this flat road with a few
obstacles the cast is not the dominant cost -- the ray and the sphere sweep
cost the same, because a sweep onto a plane converges in two advancement steps --
and the capsule sweep adds roughly half again. The per-vehicle cost grows
with the fleet because every wheel query scans every collider (one per car
plus the road), the same linear prefilter as the world queries above.

## Gameplay — vehicle force elements (17.74)

`gameplay/force_element.mojo` makes a vehicle a set of elements that each
turn the body's state into forces: `Thruster`, `aircraft.Wing` (CL / CD with
a stall blend) and `watercraft.HullBuoyancy`, which cuts the convex hull by
the water plane (`polytope_split`) for the exact submerged volume and
centroid. `test_force_elements`: the cut equals the closed-form box volume
(0.6 vs 0.6 m^3); a 0.6 m hull with a keel rights itself from 150 degrees
and a tall hull (GM < 0) falls onto its side; pre-stall lift matches
q A 2 pi alpha to 1e-4. The first table is the price of exactness against
the sampled integration of 17.28 (with each method's volume error).

## Pose interpolation (17.7)

Drawing a body between two fixed ticks. Dual-quaternion nlerp costs about
three times position-lerp + quaternion-nlerp and, in the test's screw motion
(a quarter turn about an off-centre axis), stays on the screw's circle to
~1e-7 where lerp + nlerp cuts the chord by 0.29; the PGA motor geodesic is
exact by construction and an order of magnitude dearer still (log/exp). The
runtime therefore defaults to dual-quaternion nlerp and keeps the geodesic as
the reference variant.

### Pose interpolation (17.7): cost per interpolated pose

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|
| LerpNlerp | 256 | pose | 4.58 | 218.24 | 0.01 |
| DqNlerp | 256 | pose | 12.86 | 77.75 | 0.03 |
| MotorGeodesic | 256 | pose | 232.69 | 4.30 | 0.48 |
| LerpNlerp | 4096 | pose | 4.96 | 201.69 | 0.16 |
| DqNlerp | 4096 | pose | 11.43 | 87.51 | 0.37 |
| MotorGeodesic | 4096 | pose | 195.72 | 5.11 | 6.41 |



## Destruction and fracture (17.5)

Convex decomposition is a seam: `SplitDecomposer` (hierarchical axis-plane
splits, worst part first) and `ClusterDecomposer` (k-means on voxel centres)
turn the same voxelised mesh into convex parts that cover all of it
(`test_convex_decomp`: coverage 1.0, bounded excess, identical narrowphase
verdicts on every non-grazing probe). The row labels carry the quality each
reaches: on the concave L slab both find the two-part answer at 0 % excess; on
the U slab the split needs 3 parts for 2 % excess where k-means needs 4 for
4.5 %, but the split's plane search builds a hull per candidate cut and costs
about ten times as much -- quality for build time, an offline choice versus a
quick one.

The second table is the fracture pipeline: Voronoi pre-fracture with exact
neighbour pruning (a thousand cells in well under a second), one 27-plane
runtime cut that yields a thousand pieces, adding a thousand fragments to a
`ContactScene6` as hull bodies, and the awake per-step cost of a 40-fragment
wall held together by 155 breakable welds.

### Convex decomposition variants (17.5 seam)

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|
| split | 2592 | L slab res 24: 2 parts, excess 0.000 | 836205584.00 | 0.00 | 836.21 |
| cluster | 2592 | L slab res 24: 3 parts, excess 0.000 | 112017627.00 | 0.00 | 112.02 |
| split | 2500 | U slab res 30: 3 parts, excess 0.020 | 2479155473.00 | 0.00 | 2479.16 |
| cluster | 2500 | U slab res 30: 4 parts, excess 0.045 | 171475391.00 | 0.00 | 171.48 |

## Navigation (17.14)

The navmesh bake is Recast's first half (voxelise, filter, compact, erode)
followed by per-tile greedy rectangles instead of watershed regions and
contour simplification; cost grows linearly with the span count. Of the three
pathfinders on the same queries, the polygon A* + funnel expands two orders
of magnitude fewer nodes than the 8-connected span A* and returns the shorter
any-angle path; the flow field costs one Dijkstra over every span per goal,
which only pays when many agents share the goal -- the 5000-agent table is
that case (one build plus O(1) reads against one search per agent). Carving a
dynamic obstacle recomputes blocked flags and erosion in the changed window
and rebuilds only the touched tiles; the remaining global work is the link
rebuild, which is linear in polygons. On long queries across the 128 m maze
the hierarchical search (HPA* with tiles as clusters) is not faster than the
flat polygon search at this size -- attaching start and goal to their tiles
costs about what it saves -- but it searches only the few dozen tiles on the
coarse route in detail, which is what a streamed world needs. In free 3D
space the sparse voxel octree turns open regions into single nodes: two
orders of magnitude fewer nodes expanded than the dense voxel A* for the same
smoothed path length.

### Navmesh bake (17.14): voxelise -> compact -> erode -> tile rectangles -> links

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|
| bake 32 m (374 tris, 16335 spans, 134 polys, 404 links) | 32 | bake | 16529307.00 | 0.00 | 16.53 |
| bake 64 m (1742 tris, 65325 spans, 576 polys, 1730 links) | 64 | bake | 45679466.00 | 0.00 | 45.68 |
| bake 128 m (7238 tris, 261285 spans, 2368 polys, 7298 links) | 128 | bake | 188449532.00 | 0.00 | 188.45 |

Crowd avoidance is a second seam on the same mesh. ORCA (the RVO2 linear
programs) and the social force cost about the same per agent-tick; the
sampled RVO evaluates a time-to-collision for every candidate velocity
against every neighbour and is an order of magnitude dearer. At 1024 agents
the sampled RVO also lets agents sink deepest into each other -- the
sampling misses the narrow free velocities in a jam -- while ORCA keeps the
overlap to a few centimetres (its guarantee is exact only away from walls:
the navmesh slide after the linear program breaks reciprocity).

## AI decision making and agent services (17.15)

The same guard behaviour written four ways costs very different amounts per
decision: a utility score over seven step considerations and a guarded FSM
are cheapest, the behavior tree pays for walking a reactive selector from
the root every tick, and GOAP re-plans on every decision (a real game would
cache the plan and re-plan on a world-state change). GOAP planning grows
with the domain: a 64-action chain costs tens of microseconds. The behavior
tree tick is iterative, so its cost is linear in the nodes visited and its
depth is not bounded by the call stack. The spatial services table shows the
boid step staying flat per boid from 1 000 to 10 000 fish (uniform grid), and
the brute-force perception candidate pass as the scaling item to watch.

### Decision seam (17.15): guard behaviour, 10 000 agents x 10 ticks

## Tiered simulation (17.85)

Awake two-box stacks with one observer in a corner: the policies differ in how
they pick the fine set, and all of them cut the solve to roughly a quarter of
the islands per step. The coarse tier (2 iterations over two 33 ms substeps,
every 4th step) costs about 3.5 cm of extra penetration on 0.5 m boxes. The
all-tier-0 row is the parity anchor -- bit-identical to the untiered step and,
since the island partition became linear, the same cost. At 1e4 bodies the
gain stops near 2.4x because pair collection (broadphase + narrowphase over
every body) is still paid in full every step; skipping pairs whose bodies did
not move is the next step for this item.

## Netcode strategies (17.16)

All three strategies end at the world the inputs produce offline; what they
trade is where the latency goes. Lockstep displays the world as many ticks
late as the slowest input; rollback displays the present and pays by
re-simulating every tick it mispredicted (its frame cost grows with latency,
because each late input reopens more ticks); snapshot clients do no
simulation but show the server's past, and their bandwidth is the delta
snapshots (a few hundred bytes per client per frame against a ~5 kB full
snapshot).

### Netcode strategies (17.16): 3 players, 240 ticks, 2 ticks jitter, 5 % loss

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Lockstep, latency 2 (behind 3 ticks, 760 B / frame) | 3 | frame | 163629.06 | 0.01 | 39.76 |
| Rollback ring 12, latency 2 (behind 0 ticks, 615 rollbacks, 2084 re-simulated ticks, 762 B / frame) | 3 | frame | 868855.83 | 0.00 | 209.39 |
| Snapshot interp, every 3 ticks, latency 2 (behind 7 ticks, 1794 B / frame, full snapshot 5832 B) | 3 | frame | 127285.78 | 0.01 | 32.46 |
| Lockstep, latency 6 (behind 7 ticks, 1303 B / frame) | 3 | frame | 164521.19 | 0.01 | 40.64 |
| Rollback ring 12, latency 6 (behind 0 ticks, 605 rollbacks, 4471 re-simulated ticks, 1310 B / frame) | 3 | frame | 1518327.95 | 0.00 | 365.92 |
| Snapshot interp, every 3 ticks, latency 6 (behind 15 ticks, 2234 B / frame, full snapshot 5832 B) | 3 | frame | 110440.19 | 0.01 | 28.16 |



## C-ABI boundary (12.1)

The same work driven by a C host through `build/libludens.so` and natively on
`EmbedWorld`. The step row runs a PRE system that reads and sets every 10th
box's velocity each tick (a C callback calling back into the library on the C
side); its label states whether the two worlds hash equal afterwards. The
boundary adds nothing measurable to a call (state reads are a few ns either
way); save and load pay one extra copy into or out of the host's buffer.

### C-ABI boundary (12.1): C host through libludens.so vs native EmbedWorld

## Python bindings (17.21)

The `ludens` package is ctypes over the C-ABI. A ctypes call costs a few
hundred ns per argument, so per-body calls from Python (`Body.state()`) cost
one to two µs against ~10 ns natively; the batch call (`World.poses_into`, one
call for every pose) costs the same few ns per body as native. A Python
system called once per tick is negligible next to a physics tick, so the
1000-body step with a Python system is within a few percent of native and
hashes equal.

### Python bindings (17.21): ludens package (ctypes over libludens.so) vs native EmbedWorld

| variant | N | op | ns/op | Mops/s | total ms |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|

| Python: step with a Python PRE system pushing every 10th box | 100 | tick | 1158963.49 | 0.00 | 139.08 |

| Python: World.bodies() (Body + tuple per body) | 100 | body | 667.72 | 1.50 | 13.49 |
| Python: one no-op system callback (step cost minus empty-world step) | 100 | callback | 0.00 | 0.00 | 0.00 |
| native: EmbedWorld.state per body | 100 | call | 8.57 | 116.72 | 1.71 |
